1 INTRODUCTION
1.1

Historical remarks

The finite element method (FEM) was first published
by the mathematician (Courant, 1943) on the static 2D
Saint-Venant torsion of a cylindrical bar, using linear
triangular elements. The engineers (Turner et al., 1956)
Encyclopedia of Computational Mechanics Second Edition,
Edited by Erwin Stein, René de Borst and Thomas J.R. Hughes.
© 2017 John Wiley & Sons, Ltd.
DOI: 10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025

submitted reliable shear stress fields in a delta wing of
an aircraft with the new FEM – named by them – with
2D linear triangular and bilinear quadrilateral elements.
Until today, most engineering applications of the FEM
have been made in various forms of linear elastic structural
mechanics.
The mechanical, mathematical, and computational aspects
of FEMs are presented as variants of direct or discrete
variational calculus either via local polynomial discretizations, defined on small subdomains, or with extended finite
element methods (XFEM) and the class of so-called meshfree methods, especially the reproducing kernel particle
method (RKPM). The main logical and operational steps
are illustrated in the scheme depicted in Figure 1. The
FEM is an energy-based, generalized Ritz-Galerkin approximation method with parameter-dependent test and trial
functions in related function spaces, defined in topological
subdomains.
The first challenging 2D finite element solutions using the
displacement method based on linear triangles, for example,
by Turner et al. (1956), concerned plane-stress problems
with complicated geometry and boundary conditions, which
could rarely be solved by the finite difference method (FDM).
The computers that were available in the mid-1950s, for
example, the IBM 650 or the Zuse Z21, permitted the direct
solution of about 100 linear symmetric algebraic equations
or the iterative solution of linear symmetric eigenvalue problems of the order 25, without applying special manipulations
with many intermediate inputs and outputs. The error control
of the approximate solutions could be performed with global
or sectional equilibrium conditions using integrated stresses.
Argyris and Kelsey (1960) provided (originating from
publications in 1954/1955) a systematic matrix representation of the classical force and displacement methods in linear

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Figure 1. Interaction diagram of FEM-type computational mechanics.

elastic structural analysis derived from energy principles.
The first fairly comprehensive engineering textbook on the
FEM was published by Zienkiewicz (1977).
This situation has changed considerably with the
increasing complexity of the problems as well as increasing
computing power. Nowadays, complex mechanical systems
with up to 10 or even more millions of degrees of freedom
(DOF) are solved using advanced computer technologies
and multilevel iterative solvers. A couple of internationally
experienced general-purpose programs are available and
installed in nearly every engineering consulting company.
This was accompanied with fast growing mechanical and
mathematical research including computer science.
It is obvious that it became indispensable from safety
requirements to control the discretization error using a
posteriori error estimates combined with hpd-adaptive
mesh-refinement strategies in order to obtain reliable numerical results that meet both a prescribed user-defined accuracy
at relatively low computational cost and the engineering
requirement of computational efficiency.

1.2

Mathematical foundations of the finite
element method, error estimation,
and adaptivity

The roots of bounded and efficient a posteriori error estimators can be traced back to the early contribution by Bramble
and Hilbert (1970) and the seminal work of Babuška and

Rheinboldt (1978a,b). Since then, several strategies for
explicit and implicit a posteriori error estimates have
been steadily developed within the past three decades,
covering all the basic problems encountered in structural
and solid mechanics, such as the (nearly) incompressible
and compressible finite and linearized elasticity problems
(also known as the Lamé–Navier equations).
Parallel to the first explicit residual-type a posteriori
estimator by Babuška and Miller (1987), the efficient
gradient-smoothing error indicator by Zienkiewicz and Zhu
(1987) was published, for which bounding properties were
only proved later by Carstensen and Funken (2001). For
a comprehensive overview of error estimation techniques
for elastic problems, we refer to Brenner and Scott (2002)
and Finite Element Methods. An early mathematical textbook was written by Johnson (1987) and state-of-the-art
textbooks on error estimation were presented by Verfürth
(1999), Ainsworth and Oden (2000), and Babuška and
Strouboulis (2001).
An important implicit residual-type error estimator that is
based on the solution of elementwise Neumann problems
was presented by Bank and Weiser (1985), further developed
by Ainsworth and Oden (1993), and later extended to the
finite elasticity problem by Brink and Stein (1998).
A related implicit error estimator in terms of the error
in the constitutive equation was presented by Ladevèze
and Leguillon (1983). This error estimator is based on the
construction of a kinematically admissible displacement field
and a statically admissible stress field. The upper error

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

2

bound is then achieved using the hypercircle theorem by
Prager and Synge (1947). The statically admissible stress
fields in this error estimator were derived by equilibration techniques. Parés et al. (2006), on the other hand,
created so-called flux-free local problems to bypass the
requirement of creating equilibrated tractions on the element
boundaries.
Error estimators for elastoplastic problems of the von
Mises–Hill type in structural and solid mechanics were also
developed in this period, first more with engineering techniques rather than using mathematically sound derivations.
The new chapter Mathematical Foundations of Elastoplastic Deformations in Solids treats the mathematical
aspects of computational plasticity including error analysis.
In addition, problems in fluid dynamics, such as the Stokes
problem and especially the Navier–Stokes equations, played
an important role in the development of adaptive discretization techniques. For further details, we refer to Volume 3,
ECM2, electronic edition.
All these error estimators control the finite element
discretization error in adequate norms, especially in the
energy norm. Thus, it became desirable to generalize these
error estimators to goal-oriented error measures given in
terms of arbitrary bounded linear or even nonlinear functionals such as projections of displacements, mean stress
distributions in a (small) subdomain of the elastic body,
or the J-integral as an energetic fracture criterion. Important contributions were initiated by Eriksson et al. (1995)
and developed further by Becker and Rannacher (1996),
Paraschivoiu and Patera (1998), Prudhomme and Oden
(1999), and others. Goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators are based on duality techniques as known from the
Aubin–Nitsche lemma in the a priori error analysis. Since
a norm is a canonical goal-oriented error measure, many
researchers tried to derive goal-oriented error estimators by
resorting to their global counterparts. For the linearized elasticity problem, goal-oriented error estimators were derived
by Rannacher and Suttmeier (1997), Cirak and Ramm
(1998), Strouboulis et al. (2000), Ohnimus et al. (2001), and
others. In the case of elastoplasticity, we refer to Rannacher
and Suttmeier (1999) and Cirak and Ramm (2000), and in
the case of viscoplasticity, the reader may consult (Larsson
et al., 2003). Furthermore, in elastic fracture mechanics,
goal-oriented error estimators were established by Rüter and
Stein (2006).
Although the nonlinear problem of finite elasticity plays
an important role in rubber-like and composed textile materials, error-controlled adaptive FEMs have only recently
been developed because of the specific mathematical and
algorithmic difficulties of these nonlinear problems. For error
estimators in finite elasticity based on the L2 norm, we refer
to Brink and Stein (1998), whereas goal-oriented a posteriori

3

error estimators were developed by Rüter et al. (2001) and
Bonet et al. (2002).

1.3

Advanced discretization methods

Beginning with the 1990s, generalized discretization techniques, for example, meshfree (or meshless) methods, were
developed, especially in terms of moving least-squares
(MLS) approximations. One notable representative of this
class of numerical methods is the RKPM, introduced by Liu
et al. (1995) and Chen et al. (1996). As the shape functions
are not tied to any mesh, these methods can be seen as a
generalization of mesh-based methods such as the FEM
or the XFEM, developed to model crack propagations for
fracture mechanics problems without remeshing. Despite
their many advantages in terms of not dealing with problems
resulting from a mesh and providing smooth stress approximations, meshfree methods have two major drawbacks. The
first is the accuracy of numerical integration and the second
is the fulfillment of Dirichlet boundary conditions, see Chen
et al. (1996, 2013).
Isogeometric discretization techniques – based on
B-splines – avoid numerical errors due to deviations of
the structural geometry from finite element meshes, especially with low-order polynomials. This is not discussed
in this chapter but extensively treated in the new chapter
Isogeometric Analysis: Representation of Geometry.

1.4

Reliability and safety by verification
and validation

Engineers follow the rationale “as simple as possible and
as complicated as necessary”. They move from lower to
higher complexity, e.g. for a bridge or an aircraft with
several subsystems, in order to obtain insights into effects
and numbers and to meet the general requirements of reliability and efficiency. With error-controlled adaptive methods,
we need to follow the same principle, especially in the case
of complicated and large structures with complex material
pairings, for example, thin-walled fiber-reinforced composites, reinforced concrete structures, and thin-walled highly
stiffened steel or aluminum structures.
Today and in the next decades, it is not and will not be
possible to effectively perform the necessary static and
dynamic numerical analysis for multiple loading cases,
including local and global failure states, by applying from
the beginning the most complicated mathematical models,
according to the state of the art for, for example, buckling,
inelastic deformations, and damage in all subdomains of
the whole system. Apart from that, the analysis of the
whole structure usually begins with dimensionally reduced,

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

primarily linear elastic theories of beams and plates. Those
are then expanded hierarchically with respect to the dimension, the kinematics, and the constitutive equations in
critical subdomains such as boundary layers, holes, and
supports. This is achieved using engineering knowledge
and experience or preferable by systematic error-controlled
model adaptivity based on a goal-oriented hierarchical
sequence of models derived from a reference or master
model. For papers on the development of hierarchical
models, we refer to the series of papers by Vogelius and
Babuška (1981a,b,c), to Ainsworth and Arnold (2000) for
the heat conduction problem, and to Babuška et al. (1992)
for elasticity. Model adaptivity is even more necessary
from the point of view of structural codes, based today on
semiprobabilistic safety and reliability concepts, especially
the US V& V 10-2006 by ASME (2006). For further details,
the reader may consult (Stein et al., 2004). Hence, adequate
error-controlled adaptivity of models and discretizations,
called AM-FEM, is a “must” and a new paradigm in
Computational Mechanics. Only in this manner, engineering
efficiency and safety can be achieved, taking into account
that up to about 50 loading cases can be necessary for
complicated structures.
For realizing validation with verification by model adaptivity in structural engineering, one distinguishes between
different types, which can be briefly outlined as follows:
1.

2.

3.

4.

5.

Energy-norm error estimates and adaptivity of macromodels, usually based on C1 -kinematics in space dimensions and problem-oriented constitutive equations; see,
for example, Schwab (1996), Stein and Ohnimus (1997,
1999), Ainsworth (1998), and Oden et al. (2001).
Regional model adaptivity in subdomains with boundary
layers, especially of thin-walled plates and shells using
energy-norm and regional error estimates see, for
example, Stein and Ohnimus (1997) and Stein et al.
(2004).
Goal-oriented model adaptivity of macromodels with
C1 -point continua with transition from elastic to
inelastic deformations, including dual solutions for the
far-field error, see Oden and Prudhomme (2002) and
Larsson and Runesson (2004).
Goal-oriented error estimation and adaptivity of models
for heterogeneous elastic materials and microcontinua
with homogenization to macrocontinua using representative volume elements (RVE’s), see Oden and Vemaganti (2000).
Goal-oriented coupled model and discretization error
estimation of models with different dimensions and
constitutive equations and thus with different test and
solution spaces, also called AM-FEM, see Stein et al.
(2007, 2011).

It is emphasized that the new chapter Predictive Computational Science: Computer Predictions in the Presence of
Uncertainty presents a rigorous outline for statistical uncertainty evaluation in Computational Mechanics.

1.5

Outline of the chapter

Unlike the straightforward mathematical foundation of
direct variational methods in Volume 1, mainly in the
chapters Interpolation and Quasi-Interpolation in h- and
hp-Version Finite Element Spaces and Finite Element
Methods, it has been tried here to derive and present
integrated mechanical and mathematical reasoning and
algorithms, limited by the given extent.
This chapter is divided up as follows: in Section 2, the finite
and linearized elasticity theories are briefly outlined in terms
of large strain kinematics, balance equations, and constitutive
equations. The associated boundary value problems (BVPs)
and discretizations in terms of FEMs, XFEMs, and meshfree
methods are the subject of Section 3. While Section 3 focuses
on displacement methods, Section 4 gives a brief survey on
mixed and hybrid variational problems and their discretizations. In Section 5, both energy-norm and goal-oriented a
posteriori error estimators are derived for the linearized
elasticity problem, before coupled model and discretization
error estimators and adaptivity are discussed in Section 6. In
Section 7, extensions of goal-oriented error estimators to the
case of finite elasticity are presented. The chapter concludes
with Section 8, which summarizes the major findings from
theoretical, numerical, and engineering points of view.

2

NONLINEAR AND LINEARIZED
THEORY OF ELASTICITY

In addition to rigid body mechanics, established mainly by
Galileo Galilei (1564–1642) and Isaac Newton (1643–1727)
in the seventeenth century, elastic deformations and stresses
of solid bodies as well as related systematic theories and
analytical solutions were invented over a period of about
150 years, principally from about 1685 until 1835 by
Robert Hooke (1635–1703), Leonhard Euler (1707–1783),
Joseph–Louis Lagrange (1736–1813), Augustin Louis
Cauchy (1789–1857), Claude Louis Marie Henri Navier
(1785–1836), Gabriel Lamé (1795–1870), and Siméon
Denis Poisson (1781–1840).
In order to derive the governing equations for finite elastic
deformations as well as their linearization, which yields
the elliptic Lamé–Navier partial differential equations of
linearized elasticity, we begin our studies with an outline of
large strain kinematics, followed by a collection of balance

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

4

equations in mechanics and thermodynamics. In addition,
common constitutive equations are derived by means of the
specific strain-energy function. For further details on the
theory of continuum mechanics, the reader may consult the
classics by Green and Zerna (1954), Truesdell and Noll
(1965), and Malvern (1969) as well as the more recently
published books on this topic by Bonet and Wood (1997),
Stein and Barthold (1997), Haupt (2000), and Holzapfel
(2000), whereas the works by Marsden and Hughes (1983),
Ogden (1984), and Ciarlet (1988) are devoted predominantly
to mathematical aspects of continuum mechanics.

2.1

Ω

The deformation of an elastic body

Let the elastic body be given by the closure of a bounded
open set Ω ⊂ ℝ3 with a piecewise smooth and polyhedral
boundary Γ = 𝜕Ω such that Γ = ΓD ∪ ΓN and ΓD ∩ ΓN = ∅,
where ΓD and ΓN are the portions of the boundary Γ where
Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions are imposed,
respectively. Material points of the body are denoted by the
position vector X ∈ Ω. Furthermore, let I = [t0 , t1 ] ⊂ ℝ be
the time interval of interest. Then we refer to the generally
nonlinear mapping

as motion. This mapping assigns to every pair (X, t) the
place x = 𝝓(X, t) or, more concisely, x = 𝝓t (X) in ℝ3 . Thus,
the mapping 𝝓(X, ⋅) describes the path of the material
point X ∈ Ω in ℝ3 . Moreover, for any fixed time t ∈ I,
the mapping 𝝓(⋅, t), that is, 𝝓t ∶ Ω → ℝ3 , is called deformation or synonymously configuration. Hence, a motion
is a one-parameter family of deformations with parameter t. Following Ciarlet (1988), we assume that a deformation is sufficiently smooth, injective (except possibly
on the boundary Γ to allow self-contact) and orientation
preserving.
Let us further introduce the initial (stress free) and the
current configuration of the elastic body Ω given by the
configurations 𝝓0 (Ω) at time t = t0 and 𝝓t (Ω) at arbitrary
time t ∈ (t0 , t1 ], respectively. Furthermore, for reference

Kinematics

As a point of departure, we introduce the branch of the theory
of continuum mechanics that deals with the motion of a
deformable elastic body without being concerned with its
mass and the forces that cause the motion.

2.1.2

Strain measures

In what follows, we will derive tensors that are well suited
for measuring the strain. At first glance, one might consider
using the deformation gradient F as a strain measure. If we do
this, however, we encounter several difficulties. First of all,
as mentioned earlier, F is generally unsymmetric. Moreover,
if we deal with rigid body motions, that is,
𝝓(X, t) = u(t) + Q(t) ⋅ X

∀X ∈ Ω

(9)

with Q(t) ∈ (3), we find that F = Q(t) at time t ∈ I.
Likewise, if we consider rigid body rotations x = R ⋅ X,
then we get F = R. However, strain measures should clearly
vanish in either case.
In order to derive physically more meaningful strain
measures, we consider an arbitrary (material) contravariant
line element A ∈ TX Ω (e.g., representing a fiber in an
anisotropic material). For convenience, we assume that
the fiber is of unit length, that is, ||A||2 = 1, where || ⋅ ||2
denotes the Euclidean norm. As we have seen earlier in (3a),
the vector A is mapped by F into Tx 𝝓t (Ω). Therefore, let
𝜆a = F ⋅ A be the transformed vector with ||a||2 = 1. Hence,
𝜆 ∈ ℝ denotes the stretch. With the above observations
concerning the deformation gradient F in mind, it becomes

2.2.2

The concept of mechanical stresses and stress
measures

Before we proceed with further balance equations, it is
necessary to introduce stress tensors (note that the proper
mathematical foundation of mechanical stresses goes back to
Cauchy in 1821), in order to derive local forms of the subsequent conservation laws. For this purpose, we first introduce
the traction vector t = t(x, t, n). According to the Cauchy
postulate, the traction vector t is – for C1 -manifolds – defined
in a spatial point x at time t on a surface of the current configuration 𝝓t (Ω) with associated unit outward normal n. In the
simplest case, the surface is given by the boundary 𝝓t (Γ) of
the body. More generally, the tractions t may be defined at
virtually every point x ∈ 𝝓t (Ω) by separating the body into
two parts (following Euler’s method of sections) in such a
manner that x becomes a point on a surface. The tractions t
in x at time t on the opposite sides are related by Cauchy’s
lemma, which states that t(x, t, n) = −t(x, t, −n). In other
words, Newton’s reaction principle for forces also holds
for tractions. As a consequence, by means of the equilibrium condition for the interface forces of an infinitesimal
tetrahedron, we arrive at Cauchy’s theorem, which states that

It is a trivial observation that 𝝉 is related to 𝝈 via 𝝉 = J𝝈.
Furthermore, with regard to the generalized strain measures
(15), we further define the Biot stress tensor 𝝈 B = RT ⋅ P.
We remark that within the theory of linearized elasticity,
the stress tensors P, S, 𝝉, and 𝝈 yield the same linearized
stress tensor, which is, for convenience, also referred to as 𝝈.

state of the system and not on the path taken to get to that
state. Conversely, if a property of a system is path dependent,

The Helmholtz free energy

Let us now introduce the thermodynamic potential H =
H(𝜔, t), which is referred to as the Helmholtz free energy,
defined by
H(𝜔, t) =

∫𝝓t (𝜔)

𝜚𝜓dv = U − ΘS

(51)

Here, 𝜓 = 𝜓(x, t) is the specific free energy per unit mass
as a state function. Observe that (51) is equivalent to dH =
dU − ΘdS for isothermal processes. With dU = d−W + ΘdS,
we thus obtain dH = d−W. Remarkably, the strain energy W
becomes an (extensive) state function in this case. Thus, we
deduce that H is equal (up to a constant C) to W. As a
consequence, locally we get
𝜚𝜓 = w + C

(52)

where w = w(x, t) denotes the specific strain energy per unit
volume of the current configuration 𝝓t (Ω). Following our
previous terminology, in the special case where t = 0, we
refer to the specific strain-energy function (per unit volume
of the reference configuration Ω) as w0 = w0 (X). Alternative
parameterizations of w and w0 are discussed in greater detail
in the subsequent section.

Constitutive equations

In this section, we establish constitutive equations that link
the strain tensors, and thus the displacements, with the stress
tensors.

2.3.1

The specific strain-energy function

Let us assume that the specific strain energy per unit volume
of the reference configuration Ω for elastic materials w0 =
w̆ 0 (X, F) is an isotropic tensor function of the position
vector X and of the deformation gradient F. For physical requirements, we expect limJ→0+ w̆ 0 (X, F) = +∞ and
limJ→∞ w̆ 0 (X, F) = +∞. According to the principle of frame
indifference, it is required that the specific strain-energy
function w0 fulfills
ŵ 0 (X, C) = ŵ 0 (X, Q ⋅ C ⋅ QT )

∀Q ∈ (3)

(53)

for isotropic materials. A well-known result from the
continuum theory of isotropic elastic materials states that
w0 can be expressed equivalently by the following three
invariants:
𝜄1 = tr C
1

2
𝜄3 = det C

takes the form w0 = ŵ 0 (X, 𝜾), where 𝜾 = (𝜄1 , 𝜄2 , 𝜄3 ). Note that
this specific strain-energy function is confined to compressible materials only.

3

VARIATIONAL FORMULATIONS AND
THEIR DISCRETIZATIONS

With the specific strain-energy function w0 for isotropic
materials at hand, accompanied by the associated stress
tensors and tensors of elastic tangent moduli, we are now in
a position to introduce the nonlinear BVP of isotropic finite
hyperelasticity. Furthermore, the Lamé–Navier equations of
linearized elasticity are obtained by a linearization of the
underlying nonlinear problem around the reference configuration. Subsequently, we briefly discuss the appropriate
design of finite-dimensional solution and test spaces h
based on both mesh-based and meshfree methods, in order
to derive approximate solutions of the (non)linear variational problems. The mesh-based methods discussed in this
chapter are the FEM and modifications such as the XFEM.
The representative of meshfree methods, see Meshfree
Methods, is the RKPM as elaborated in Reproducing
Kernel Particle Method for Solving Partial Differential
Equations. In this chapter, we keep to the essentials and
do not aim to present a comprehensive overview of FEM,
XFEM, or RKPM, which is discussed in greater detail in the
vast literature available, for example, in the case of FEM in
the classics by Hughes (1987), Szabó and Babuška (1991),
Bathe (1996), and Zienkiewicz and Taylor (2000) as well
as in the mathematically oriented books by Ciarlet (1978),
Schwab (1998), Brenner and Scott (2002), and Braess
(2007). With regard to the nonlinear variational problem,
we refer to the works by Crisfield (1997), Belytschko
et al. (2000), and Wriggers (2001), since they are mainly
concerned with FEMs for nonlinear problems.

3.1

The boundary value problem

Let us begin our studies by establishing the BVPs in weak
and strong forms for both finite and linearized elasticity.

3.1.1

Weak forms

Assuming homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions, all
admissible displacements u ∶ Ω → ℝ3 of the elastic body Ω

Under certain restrictions on w0 , Ball (1977) has established
a mathematical existence theory for the above nonlinear
variational problem. He proved that there exists a solution
to the problem of minimizing the total potential energy Π
under the assumption that the specific strain energy w0 is
polyconvex; see also Ciarlet (1988). Roughly speaking, this
means that w0 is convex in each variable, where it should
be noted that polyconvexity is weaker than convexity. The
uniqueness of the solution, however, cannot be expected
in general, since it is easily verified that we typically deal
with several solutions after stability points, that is, after
bifurcation or limit points.

3.2

Finite-dimensional variational problems

We introduce a finite-dimensional space h ⊂ , that is,
a subspace of , using either a mesh-based or a meshfree
method, without going into details of the construction at
this stage. The construction of such a space is discussed in
Sections 3.3–3.5. Then, in the finite-dimensional discretization of the nonlinear variational problem (89) and the linear
variational problem (94), we seek a discrete solution uh ∈ h
with the use of admissible test functions vh ∈ h such that
a(uh , vh ) = F(vh )

∀vh ∈ h

(105)

Note that in the case of the nonlinear problem (89), only the
existence of the discrete solution uh ∈ h can be guaranteed
(assuming that h ⊂ ), whereas if (105) represents the
discretization of the linear problem (94), then clearly a
unique solution uh ∈ h exists as well.

3.2.1

The iterative Newton–Raphson scheme

3.2.2

Because the discretized problem (105) is nonlinear in
the case of finite elasticity, we next briefly introduce the
well-known iterative Newton–Raphson scheme. Therefore, we first consider an arbitrary solution uh ∈ h on the
(discrete) solution path of the finite-dimensional problem
(105). Within a Newton step, we then search for an increment

Test and solution spaces

To obtain a numerical solution of the finite and linearized
elasticity problems, it remains to discuss how the
finite-dimensional space h is constructed in terms of
both mesh-based and meshfree methods. In either case, the
idea is to introduce nn nodes Xi ∈ Ω and to create shape
functions Ni (X) associated with each of the nodes, which
are usually termed particles in the framework of meshfree
methods. The finite-dimensional space h is thus defined as
{ }n
h = span Ni i n
and a function vh ∈ h can be expressed as

Δ

that is, the Gâteaux derivative of a with respect to the first
argument. A visualization of the tangent form can be seen in
Figure 44. Furthermore, the functional Ru ∶ h → ℝ denotes
the weak form of the residual given as
Ru (uh ; vh ) = F(vh ) − a(uh , vh )

(108)

Thus, after solving (106), we are led to a “better” solution
Δ
uh + uh ∈ h that is “closer” to the finite element solution
uh ∈ h in terms of smaller residuals (measured in a suitable
norm).
It is worth noticing that if uh is set to 𝟎, which is usually
the starting point for the Newton–Raphson algorithm, it turns
out that the first Newton step is obviously the discretization
of the variational form of the linearized elasticity problem
(94), since Ru (𝟎; vh ) = F(vh ).
Furthermore, we would like to point out that the classical
Newton–Raphson algorithm has the merit that it provides
a quadratic convergence rate in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the solution, whereas related algorithms such as
the quasi-Newton method require less computational efforts
(because the tangent does not change within the iterations),
but the rapid quadratic convergence rate can often not be
retained. However, the Newton–Raphson algorithm is not
necessarily globally convergent, that is, as with any iterative
method, the convergence behavior strongly depends on
the starting point. For further details, we refer to Iterative
Solutions of Nonlinear Systems, Solution Manifolds, and
Bifurcations.

(109)

vh (X) =

∑

Ni (X)̂vi

∀X ∈ Ω

(110)

nn

where v̂ i is a nodal coefficient.
The main difference between mesh-based and meshfree
methods is thus the construction of the shape functions Ni
and its consequences on the discretized variational problems. Briefly speaking, in mesh-based methods, the shape
functions Ni are constructed so that they vanish at all other
nodes, that is, Ni (Xj ) = 𝛿ij , which is also referred to as
Kronecker-delta property. In meshfree methods, however,
the shape functions Ni do not possess the Kronecker-delta
property, that is, Ni (Xj ) ≠ 𝛿ij . A direct consequence is that in
the case of meshfree methods, Dirichlet boundary conditions
cannot be satisfied by functions vh ∈ h (and thus h ⊄ ),
whereas for mesh-based methods, they are easily satisfied.
This goes along with the meaning of the nodal coefficient v̂ i ,
which is generally only in the case of mesh-based methods
also the value of vh evaluated at node Xi .
Once the discrete functions vh are established, they need
be integrated within the weak form (105). Although other
methods are possible, this is most often carried out using
Gauss integration owing to the many advantages of this
method. This requires the construction of a mesh for both
mesh-based and meshfree methods. In mesh-based methods,
it turns out that the shape functions Ni (X) can be tied to the
mesh so that every element of the mesh can be described
using the same shape functions. In meshfree methods,
however, the shape functions are not tied to the mesh,
which eliminates the numerical problems that may arise
from a distorted mesh. It should be briefly mentioned at
this stage that another advantage of having a mesh that is
independent from the shape functions is adaptivity as nodes
can be added and even be removed from the discretization
easily, see Section 5.9.5. It should further be pointed out
that in meshfree methods, a mesh is required for integration
purposes only.

3.3

The finite element method (FEM)

In the following sections, we give several examples of how
the finite-dimensional space h can be constructed in terms
of shape functions emanating from different numerical
approaches. In this section, we begin with mesh-based
methods, more specifically with FEM. We also present
the shape functions for XFEM and meshfree methods in
Sections 3.4 and 3.5, respectively. For a systematic presentation of interpolation polynomials as shape functions for
FEM, we refer to Interpolation and Quasi-Interpolation
in h- and hp-Version Finite Element Spaces.
In order to establish the FEM, we first introduce a partition  of Ω that is given by a finite number of closed
subdomains Ωe = Ωe ∪ 𝜕Ωe with nonempty interior, that is,
⋃
Ω = Ω ∈ Ωe . These subdomains are called elements.
e

Furthermore, the intersection of two adjacent elements Ωe
and Ωa is supposed to be either a vertex, an edge, or a face
if Ωe ∩ Ωa ≠ ∅, and that a family of partitions {} is locally
quasi-uniform, cf., for example, Ainsworth and Oden (2000).
We further establish polynomial spaces on these elements.
According to the notation by Ciarlet (1978), let Pk (Ωe )
denote the space of polynomials of total degree ≤ k
with dimension dim Pk (Ωe ) = (1∕6)(k + 1)(k + 2)(k + 3).
Moreover, let Qk (Ωe ) denote the space of polynomials,
which are of degree ≤ k with respect to each variable, with
dimension dim Qk (Ωe ) = (k + 1)3 . Then, Σ is defined as
the set of DOF given by the values at the nodes Xn ∈ Ωe
with 1 ≤ n ≤ dim Pk (Ωe ) or 1 ≤ n ≤ dim Qk (Ωe ).

3.3.1

Lagrangian interpolation polynomials

In the 1960s, node- and elementwise h-discretizations (h
is the characteristic element length) based on Lagrangian
polynomials were first used. For a given set of distinct points
𝜉j and values 𝜂j , the Lagrangian interpolation polynomial is
the one of least degree that takes the given value 𝜂j at point
𝜉j for each j. The interpolating polynomial of least degree is
unique with the drawback that a change of the coordinate 𝜉j
requires a recalculation of the interpolant.

For k distinct data points (𝜉1 , 𝜂1 ), … , (𝜉j , 𝜂j ), … , (𝜉k , 𝜂k ),
the Lagrangian interpolation polynomial is a linear combination of the Lagrangian basis

a function is quadratic by construction. The same principle
holds for higher order Lagrangian basis functions.

The element shape functions Ni for each element Ωe of

with each node. This holds for 1D elements as depicted in
Figure 3 but also for several space dimensions as depicted in
Figure 12(a) for the case of two space dimensions.
Furthermore, it is possible to construct the Lagrangian
basis functions hierarchically. As an example, the quadratic
element shape functions can be constructed by the linear
ones adding a quadratic one. In this way, the lower order
shape functions are included in the higher order basis. This is
an important concept that is used in the so-called p-version
of FEM and was first published by Babuška et al. (1981).
Further hierarchical shape functions, based on Legendre
polynomials, are the subject of the following section.

Triangular elements

Next, we present suitable shape functions for triangular
elements, as the simplest 2D elements, using homogeneous
area or baricentric coordinates 𝜆1 , 𝜆2 , and 𝜆3 , see Melosh
(1963). Note that this concept can be easily extended to 3D
tetrahedral elements.
Using area coordinates, a point P in the triangle is given
by the coordinates 𝜆1 = A1 ∕A, 𝜆2 = A2 ∕A, and 𝜆3 = A3 ∕A,
as can be seen in Figure 7. The coordinates have the property
𝜆1 + 𝜆2 + 𝜆3 = 1 and thus we have two independent coordinates only.

3.3.7

Anisotropic finite elements

Beams, plates, and shells are thin-walled structures by definition. Owing to the kinematic hypothesis “plane cross sections
remain plane after elastic deformations”, which can be
traced back to Johann Bernoulli (1667–1748), Gustav Robert
Kirchhoff (1824–1887), and Augustus Edward Hough Love
(1863–1940), linear displacement test and trial functions
are introduced in the thickness direction for the horizontal
displacements. However, for the deflections w, polynomials
of higher order are more efficient, provided that we have
sufficient regularity of the analytical solution.
This can be easily verified by means of a simple example.
Let us therefore consider the Bernoulli cantilever beam
subjected to a transverse loading at the free end as depicted
in Figure 5. The homogeneous differential equation for the
Bernoulli beam, see (120) for p = 0, has a cubic polynomial
as the homogeneous solution. The associated finite element
discretization of the variational form of (120) based on an
anisotropic plane-stress displacement Q3 -Q1 elements, that
is, with a cubic polynomial in the axial direction and a linear
polynomial in the thickness direction, yields the exact beam
solution for the maximum bending stress at the clamped end
with one element only, that is, with 12 DOF. Note that owing
to symmetry conditions, only one half of the system needs to
be modeled. The corresponding isotropic Q3 element leads
to the same result, but here 24 DOF are required. Moreover,
the analytical solution is approximated with high accuracy
using isotropic Q2 elements, but in this case, 12 elements
(with two elements in the thickness direction) with 120 DOF
are required. If we further reduce the polynomial order, that
is, if we use 20 Q1 elements with again 120 DOF, then we
only reach 75% of the maximum bending stress. This shows
the efficiency of problem-oriented anisotropic test and trial
functions for thin-walled structures.

3.4

Γc

Ω
ΓD

Figure 9. Precracked polygonal domain Ω.

extension of the FEM, as presented in the preceding sections,
toward problems with (propagating) discontinuities such
as cracks. Therefore, discontinuous functions are added to
the finite element shape functions along with enrichment
functions near singularities or, more general, near areas in
which the solution has steep gradients such as the vicinity
of the crack tip in fracture mechanics. This approach is thus
still mesh based. However, the mesh is not aligned with the
discontinuity that allows, for example, to simulate arbitrary
crack propagation with minimal remeshing. Furthermore,
the mesh is usually chosen to be coarse, since the surplus
of accuracy in the numerical solution is obtained by the
enrichment functions.
For the sake of simplicity, we confine ourselves in
this chapter to applying XFEM to linear elastic fracture
mechanics (LEFM) in two space dimensions only, that
is, using either a plane-strain or plane-stress assumption
as presented in Section 2.4. In fracture mechanics, we
insert a crack into the elastic body Ω ⊂ ℝ2 as introduced
in Section 2.1.1. As shown in Figure 9, its boundary Γ =
𝜕Ω now consists of three disjoint parts such that Γ =
ΓD ∪ ΓN ∪ Γc , where Γc is a traction-free boundary that
models the preexisting crack. More precisely, it is a

In recent years, it became more and more popular to approximate the exact solutions of problems in fracture mechanics
by the XFEM as introduced and developed further by
Belytschko and Black (1999), Moës et al. (1999), Fries
(2008), and others, see also Extended Finite Element
Methods. As the name implies, XFEM can be seen as an

For the XFEM discretization of the continuous problem

Under the framework of partition of unity, the XFEM

The first part is the standard approximation as known from
the finite element method and as introduced in the preceding
sections. The second part handles the crack that can run
through an element and the third part improves the near
crack tip approximation (which is redundant if an adaptive
mesh-refinement scheme is used at the crack tip). More
precisely, the XFEM approximation is expressed as an

, whereas I ∗ ⊂ I and J ∗ ⊂ I are the subsets of nodes that are
enriched by the enrichment functions. In general, I ∗ consists
of all nodes within a certain radius rtip around the crack tip
and J ∗ consists of the closest neighboring nodes to the ones in
I ∗ . Lastly, IH ⊂ I is the subset of nodes that are enriched for
the crack discontinuity (i.e., it consists of nodes of elements
that are cut by the crack excluding the nodes belonging to I ∗ ).
It is worth noticing that the XFEM approximation
(146) affects the way in which the unknown solution u
is discretized only. Therefore, it is generally possible to
apply XFEM to both finite elasticity (with a different set of
enrichment functions) and linearized elasticity problems as
introduced in Section 3.1.1 for both 2D and 3D problems.
We note further that again an isoparametric mapping can be
used as introduced in Section 3.3.5.

3.5

The reproducing kernel particle method
(RKPM)

As mentioned earlier in this section, the shape functions
to construct an approximate solution uh of the variational
problem (105) do not necessarily have to be tied to a mesh
as is the case for FEM and its variations, such as XFEM, as
presented in Sections 3.3 and 3.4, respectively. In general,
numerical methods based on shape functions that are not
tied to a mesh are referred to as meshfree methods. In this

chapter, however, we do not aim at presenting an overview of
meshfree methods. Rather, we focus on RKPM as one of its
representatives. For a survey of meshfree methods, we refer
the interested reader to Liu et al. (1996) and Reproducing
Kernel Particle Method for Solving Partial Differential
Equations.
The reproducing kernel (RK) shape function Ni is based on
a kernel function Φ, typically chosen as a cubic B-spline, that
takes care of the smoothness of the approximation and thus
allows smooth gradient fields such as stresses and strains. It
should be noted in this context that mesh-based methods, on
the other hand, generally yield discontinuous gradient fields
with jumps on the interelement boundaries. For consistency,
the kernel function Φ needs to be corrected by a correction
function C. Thus, the RK shape function associated with a
particle Xi takes the form
Ni (X) = Φ(X − Xi )C(X; X − Xi )

(152)

The correction function C is defined as
C(X; X − Xi ) = b(X)T H(X − Xi )

(153)

in terms of (unknown) coefficients b and vector of monomial
basis functions H. The coefficients b are determined by
the property that the RK shape functions Ni can exactly
reproduce pth-order polynomials
∑

= x1i x2 · · · xdk , i + j + · · · + k = 0, 1, … , p

(154)

and become
b(X) = M−1 (X)H(𝟎)

(155)

with the symmetric moment matrix
M(X) =

∑
nP

Φ(X − XI ) H(X − XI ) HT (X − XI )

(156)

Note that since M needs to be invertible, the support of the
kernel function Φ, that is, supp Φ, needs to cover a sufficient
amount of particles. Now it also becomes clear that supp Φ
needs to be increased if the order p of the polynomials that
need to be reproduced is increased. We further remark that
in a practical computation of M(X), the sum in (156) needs
only to be taken over those particles Xi the shape function’s
supports of which cover the evaluation point X, that is,
X ∈ supp Ni .
Inserting the coefficients (155) into (153) and the result into
(152), we arrive at the RK shape function representation
Ni (X) = Φ(X − Xi )H(𝟎)T M−1 (X)H(X − Xi )

(157)

which is independent from any mesh. However, a standardization of shape functions, as introduced in Section 3.3.5
using the isoparametric concept, is hard to achieve with the
given RK shape functions and thus typically the shape functions need to be constructed separately for each particle based
on the coefficients (155). Furthermore, depending on the size
of supp Ni , the associated stiffness matrix is more densely
populated. These differences to the FEM thus result in higher
computational costs. A general comparison of finite element
(FE) and RK shape functions is visualized in Figure 12(a)
and (b), respectively.
We further remark that whenever gradients of the shape
functions are required, for example, to build the stiffness
matrix (144), in this chapter we make use of direct differentiation of the shape functions Ni , which leads to exact but
computationally expensive derivatives.
As mentioned earlier, the fulfillment of Dirichlet boundary
conditions on ΓD is not pertinent to the meshfree method,
since the test and trial functions are in h ⊄ . Thus,
Dirichlet boundary conditions need to be fulfilled in an alternative way. Various approaches have been presented over the
years to deal with this issue. The most common ones are to

Without loss of generality, here we make use of Nitsche’s
method to weakly impose the homogeneous Dirichlet
boundary conditions (103a). In this way, we bypass the
use of mixed methods with associated stability problems
and additional DOF as obtained by the Lagrange multiplier method. Note also that Nitsche’s method includes the
penalty method as a special case.
Furthermore, we restrict our considerations in this section
to linear elliptic problems such as the linearized elasticity
problem as introduced in Section 3.1.1. It should be pointed
out, however, that also nonlinear problems such as the
finite elasticity problem, as also introduced in Section 3.1.1,
can be solved approximately using meshfree methods in
virtually the same way as elaborated for the case of FEM in
Section 3.3.
In Nitsche’s method, the weak fulfillment of Dirichlet
boundary conditions yields the following modified version of
the discrete problem (105): find the RKPM solution uh ∈ h
such that
ah (uh , vh ) = F(vh ) ∀vh ∈ h

(158)

Here, the discretization-dependent (Nitsche) symmetric
bilinear form ah is defined, in the general case, on  ×  as

ah (uh , vh ) = a(uh , vh ) −

v ⋅ 𝝈(uh ) ⋅ n dA
∫ΓD h

− uh ⋅ 𝝈(vh ) ⋅ n dA

h ∫ΓD h h

(159)
with meshfree discretization parameter h and penalty parameter 𝛽 ∈ ℝ+ that takes the role of a stabilization parameter.
Existence and uniqueness of the RKPM solution uh is
not a direct consequence of the existence and uniqueness
considerations of the associated continuous problem, since
the bilinear form is modified. To show that ah is continuous and coercive and thus stable, discretization-dependent
norms are required. For further details in the framework of finite elements, the reader may consult Stenberg
(1995). It should be noted, however, that the proof of coercivity relies on inverse estimates, which are not proven
yet for meshfree methods and thus are only assumed
to hold.
Lastly, we remark that since the RK shape functions
Ni , as defined in (157), are rational functions and since
their supports overlap, domain integration of the bilinear
and linear forms ah and F, respectively, becomes an issue.
The resulting integration error is assumed to be sufficiently
small throughout this chapter. This can be achieved, for
example, while using a sufficiently high-order Gauss quadrature rule. Alternatively, the integration error can be, at best,
corrected in terms of a variationally consistent integration
(VCI) method as recently proposed by Chen et al. (2013) and
applied to a nodal integration technique, to retain the meshfree character of the method, known as stabilized nonconforming nodal integration (SNNI) by Rüter et al. (2012).

4

MIXED AND HYBRID METHODS IN
LINEARIZED ELASTICITY

The (one-field) displacement method as presented in
Sections 3.3 and 3.4 for the FEM and the XFEM, respectively, has the significant drawback that the approximation
of the stress field may have jumps on the interelement
boundaries. This is because the stress field can only be
obtained by a postprocess that requires the derivatives of the
shape functions according to (69). Moreover, for lower order
elements, we may encounter numerical instabilities, such
as the locking phenomenon in the case of (nearly) incompressible materials. To cope with these problems, a meshfree
method as presented in Section 3.5 proves advantageous
or, if a mesh-based method is preferred, mixed and hybrid
FEMs can be used as briefly outlined in this section. For
further studies on mixed FEMs, we refer to Mixed Finite
Element Methods.

4.1

A dual-mixed two-field finite method based
on the Hellinger–Reissner functional

Next, we derive a two-field variational problem based on
the well-known Hellinger–Reissner functional (named after
Hellinger, 1914; Reissner, 1950) and the associated FEM. In
order to derive the Hellinger–Reissner functional by means

∗

Hybrid stress methods

To begin with, we briefly introduce the hybrid stress FEM
as introduced by Pian (1964) for 2D linearized elasticity,
which is based on the complementary Dirichlet energy principle. This principle is also used for the finite element stress
approach by de Veubecke (1964) and others in the 1960s
and 1970s. In the associated pure stress approach, the test
and trial stresses must be statically admissible in the sense
of a strong fulfillment of the equilibrium equations (102a)
and the Neumann boundary conditions (103b). It was shown
that the simplest plane-stress triangular element needs polynomials of fifth degree for the stresses 𝜎xx , 𝜎yy , and 𝜏xy with
3 × 21 = 63 parameters, which are reduced by the equilibrium conditions to 32 parameters according to the balancing
of each polynomial term, which is of no practical interest.
Pian (1964) relaxed the continuity conditions of the tractions
by adding these terms multiplied by Lagrange multipliers
𝝀 to the complementary energy functional. The Lagrange
multipliers 𝝀 result in the boundary displacements uh |𝜕Ωe .
Again, the element flexibility matrix Ae can be eliminated on
the element level. This method was developed for triangular
and quadrilateral 2D plane-stress elements and for corresponding plate-bending elements.

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Again, it should be noted that in order to prove the existence and uniqueness of the finite element solution (𝝈 h , uh ) ∈
h × h , one crucial point is that the finite-dimensional
subspaces h and h are well balanced in the sense that the
bilinear form b fulfills the inf-sup condition
b(𝝉 h , vh )
≥𝛽
||𝝉
h || ||vh ||
h

inf sup

vh ∈h 𝝉 ∈
h

(178)

with 𝛽 > 0 and independent of the mesh width h. In the
discrete finite element setting, the above inf-sup condition is also referred to as the Babuška-Brezzi condition
according to the seminal work by Babuška (1973a) and
Brezzi (1974); see also Brezzi and Fortin (1991) and Mixed
Finite Element Methods. We note, in particular, that (178)
is a global condition and therefore, balancing the DOF
on the element or patch level, for example by means of a
local eigenvalue analysis, is a necessary but not a sufficient
condition. In addition, counting and balancing the number
of unknowns on patch or even global level – where the
number of DOF associated with the stress field has to
be larger than the corresponding displacement DOF – is
not sufficient. Consequently, numerous mechanically
motivated patch tests, which are available in the engineering literature (see e.g., Zienkiewicz and Taylor, 2000),
are of practical use but cannot claim to be sufficient in
general.
The Hellinger–Reissner type mixed FEM is of particular
importance for rigorous stability proofs, especially for linear
test and trial functions in the displacement FEM giving rise to
locking phenomena, such as (nearly) incompressible elastic
deformations as well as for shear elastic Timoshenko beams
and for Reissner–Mindlin plates and shells. Underintegrations of stiffness matrices with the associated patch test (see
Taylor et al., 1986) and stabilizing enhanced assumed strain
(EAS) methods (see Simo and Rifai, 1990) are also mathematically based on related mixed methods and their stability
conditions, see Braess (1998).
The reason for the fact that a proper mixed method prevents
nonregular global stiffness matrices is the controlled softening of energy due to relaxed requirements on the geometric
equations.
As we have seen, however, the mixed approach presented
does generally not yield a continuous stress field. Furthermore, with the mixed method, we still encounter difficulties in (nearly) incompressible elasticity. To cope with these
problems, another pairing of test and solution spaces is
required, namely, H(div, Ω) and L2 (Ω) instead of  and ,
which leads to continuous stress fields but discontinuous
displacements fields.

4.3

A three-field formulation based on the
Hu–Washizu functional

Rather than two independent unknown fields 𝝈 and u, as used
in the Hellinger–Reissner functional (166), we now introduce
an additional unknown strain field 𝜺 ∈ , which leads to the
well-known Hu–Washizu functional due to Hu (1955) and
Washizu (1955).
The Hu–Washizu functional ΠHW ∶  ×  ×  → ℝ
– with  and  defined as  =  = [L2 (Ω)]3×3 in the
simplest case, whereas  is defined as in (83) – can be easily
derived from the weak forms of the constitutive equation
(102b), the equilibrium equation (102a), and the kinematic
equation (102c), which turn out to be the variations of
ΠHW with respect to the strain, displacement, and stress
fields. In other words, we have D𝜺 ΠHW (𝜺, u, 𝝈) ⋅ 𝜼 = 0,
Du ΠHW (𝜺, u, 𝝈) ⋅ v = 0, and D𝝈 ΠHW (𝜺, u, 𝝈) ⋅ 𝝉 = 0 or,
synonymously, 𝛿𝜺 ΠHW = 0, 𝛿u ΠHW = 0, and 𝛿𝝈 ΠHW = 0.

The above two-field formulation (189) reminds on the dualmixed FEM as presented in Section 4.2. Indeed, Stolarski

Hellinger–Reissner formulation with the same spaces h
and h .
Note again that in the case of (nearly) incompressible elasticity, other pairings of test and solution spaces are required.
Lastly, we remark that there exist further mixed functionals
and associated FEMs, which are balanced on the element
level and also provide global numerical stability, in particular
the so-called PEERS (plane elasticity element with reduced
symmetry) element (see Arnold et al., 1984; Stenberg, 1988)
and the class of BDM elements (see Brezzi et al., 1985;
Braess et al., 1995). These elements are discussed in detail
in Mixed Finite Element Methods.

4.5

The finite element exterior calculus

In this additional section, selected results of the seminal
work of Arnold et al. (2006, 2009, 2010), Arnold (2013) on
the finite element exterior calculus for the generalized setting
of stable mixed FEMs are briefly outlined. The finite element
exterior calculus combines the tools of algebraic topology
with the methods of numerical analysis to construct discrete
differential operators that are compatible with the geometrical, algebraic, and topological structures of their continuous
counterparts, thereby ensuring the stability of the discrete
BVP, that is, the problem fulfills the inf-sup conditions of
classical mixed methods. The finite element exterior calculus
unifies many previously-discovered finite elements such as
the Lagrange, Raviart–Thomas, Brezzi–Douglas–Marini,
Nédélec, and discontinuous Galerkin element families

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

into a vastly more general theory. Among numerous other
applications, it delivers a stable discretization of mixed
linearized elasticity to arbitrary polynomial orders and in
higher spatial dimensions, a problem which had previously
been unsolved. It also provides the framework for error analysis of the considered mixed problems. In the following, an
overview of the general theory, of applications to linearized
elasticity, as well as a recipe for the construction of the
finite element basis forms and the application to linearized
elasticity is given.

4.5.1

Exterior calculus

As a preliminary step, it is necessary to recall the fundamentals of the exterior calculus of differential forms on manifolds, from which the method derives its name, beginning
with the definition of a differential form and its associated
algebra induced by the wedge product.
A scalar-valued differential k-form is totally antisymmetric, that is, antisymmetric
with respect to all permutations
( )
of the indices, 0k -tensor, defined by the alternating multilinear map
TP  × · · · × TP  → ℝ

where TP  is the tangent space at point P of the oriented
n-dimensional manifold . For k = 0, the map is  → ℝ.
A differential k-form can thus be expressed in local coordinate form as the linear combinations of k-fold wedges of the
tangent basis covectors dxi ∈ TP∗ , that is, a local basis of
differential k-forms can be defined as
{dx𝝈 |𝝈 ∈ Σ(k, n)},

( )

When the set Σ(k, n) is clear from context, Einstein’s summation convention can be employed to simplify notation by

The space of differential k-forms Λk (TP∗ ) is a vector
space together with the exterior (wedge) product ∧ ∶ Λk ×
Λp → Λk+p , which has the properties
𝝎 ∧ 𝝂 = (−1)kp 𝝂 ∧ 𝝎

(194)

and for k odd, 𝝎 ∧ 𝝎 = 0. Note that for k = 1, the space of
differential k-forms Λ1 (TP∗ ) is simply the cotangent space
TP∗ . The algebra generated by the exterior product obeys
the rules of the determinant and thus follows arguments of
geometry, making it a useful tool in studying higher dimensional analogs of oriented areas and volumes. Therefore,
differential forms can be used in defining physical quantities with orientation, such as flux or circulation. It is also
possible to formulate the geometric analog of continuum
mechanics, in which mechanical stress is considered to be
a vector-valued n − 1-differential form. For more on the
geometric aspects of physics problems, see, for example, the
textbook of Frankel (2011).

of the Poisson problem on a domain Ω ⊂ ℝn . Here, p is a

The Hodge Laplacian d𝜹 + 𝜹d is the generalization of the
Laplace operator to differential k-forms and leads to the
formulation
(d𝜹 + 𝜹d)u = f − p,

which is a central concept in the finite element exterior
calculus. For f = 0, the solutions to the problem given above
are simply the functions in ℌk . While the problem with the
Lagrange multiplier is valid for any forcing term f , the same
problem without the Lagrange multiplier would require
f ⊥ ℌk in order for the solution to exist. Note that Pℌk f = 0
when f ⊥ ℌk . However, even for vanishing harmonic forms,
the finite element solution of the primal problem does
not converge, as the approximate solution lies in a closed
subspace of the solution space, which does not contain the
exact solution.
This difficulty is overcome by considering the mixed
formulation of the Hodge Laplacian

coderivative lowers the degree of the differential form.

To put the latter into the familiar context of partial differential equations, the case of n = 3 dimensions and k = 3 is
considered, for which it is possible to identify HΛ2 (Ω) with
H(div, Ω, ℝ3 ) and HΛ3 (Ω) with L2 (Ω). Thus the considered
problem is the standard mixed formulation of the Dirichlet
problem for the scalar Poisson equation with 𝝈 = −grad u
and boundary condition u = 0. Seeking 𝝈 ∈ H0 (div, Ω, ℝ3 )
substitutes the boundary condition u = 0 for 𝝈 ⋅ n = 0. If, on
the other hand, k = 0, it is possible to identify HΛ−1 (Ω) with
0 and HΛ0 (Ω) with H 1 (Ω), leading to the Neumann problem
of the scalar Poisson equation in primal form, that is, with
𝜎 = 0. Seeking u ∈ H01 (Ω) gives the corresponding Dirichlet
problem. The intermediate cases k = 1 and k = 2, with 𝝈 =
−div u and 𝝈 = curl u, respectively, yield the two possible
mixed formulations of the vectorial Poisson problem.

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

4.5.3

Stable discretization of mixed linearized
elasticity

The finite element exterior calculus provides stable finite
element discretization of the mixed elasticity problem
for both displacement and stress boundary conditions. It
is remarked that there are two approaches to the finite
element solution of mixed elasticity: (i) imposing the
symmetry of stress in a strong sense by restricting the
solution space or (ii) imposing symmetry weakly by
introducing a Lagrange multiplier. In approximating
the mixed form with strong symmetry, however, spaces
discretizing the square-integrable symmetric tensor fields
𝝈 ∈ H(div, Ω, sym 𝕄) have proven difficult to find, as,
contrary to the case of weak symmetry, the elasticity
complex with strongly imposed symmetry cannot be derived
directly from the de Rham complex. The following summary
will therefore limit itself to the formulation with weakly
imposed symmetry. It will also neglect the case of traction
boundary conditions, as their treatment goes beyond the
introductory presentation attempted here. In the language of
partial differential equations, the mixed elasticity problem
𝔻 ∶ 𝝈 = gradsym u, −div 𝝈 = b in Ω, u = 𝟎 on 𝜕Ω
(206)
where b denotes the volume-specific body-force, in variational form with weakly imposed symmetry can be stated as:
find (𝝈, u, p) ∈ H(div, Ω, 𝕄) × L2 (Ω, ℝn ) × L2 (Ω, skw 𝕄)
such that
∫Ω

(𝝈 ∶ 𝔻 ∶ 𝝉 + div𝝉 ⋅ u + 𝝉 ∶ p) dV = 0
∀𝝉 ∈ H(div, Ω, 𝕄)
−

∫Ω

div𝝈 ⋅ vdV =

∫Ω

b ⋅ v dV
∫Ω
∀v ∈ L2 (Ω, ℝn )

𝝈 ∶ q dV = 0
∀q ∈ L2 (Ω, skw 𝕄) (207)

Here, 𝔻 is the compliance tensor, that is, 𝔻 ≡ C−1 when C
exists, 𝕄 denotes the field of second-order tensors, and skw𝕄
denotes the field of skew-symmetric second-order tensors.
The interpretation of the weak form goes as follows: as
𝝉 ∶ grad u = −div𝝉 ⋅ u when u = 0 on 𝜕Ω, the first equation
enforces the equality of “physical strains” 𝝈 ∶ 𝔻 and “geometric strains” (grad u)sym . The second equation ensures the
conservation of linear momentum and the third equation
ensures the conservation of angular momentum, that is, the
symmetry of the stress tensor.
In order to appropriately discuss the generalized mixed
elasticity problem in the framework of differential forms,

it is first necessary to extend the notion of differential
forms to vector-valued coefficients. Denote the space of
differential k-forms with coefficients in a vector space W

Having introduced vector-valued differential k-forms, it
is possible to define Cauchy stress 𝝈 as a vector-valued
n − 1-differential form in Λn−1 (Ω, ℝn ). Then, it intuitively
follows that integration of stress across a hypersurface of
dimension n − 1 gives the force vector in ℝn . It is remarked
that ⋆𝝈 ∈ Λ1 (Ω, ℝn ) is the standard notion of mechanical
stress, that is, it defines a linear operator ℝn → ℝn , which
takes the normal vector as an argument and yields the stress
vector. Depending on definition, ⋆𝝈 ∈ Λ1 (Ω, ℝn ) can then
be identified with a second-order tensor. The definition of 𝝈
itself can analogously be interpreted as taking vectors in the

The finite element exterior calculus establishes that a stable

r Λn−1 (h , ℝn ) for stress, r−1 Λn (h , ℝn ) for displacement,

4.5.4

Construction of the finite element basis forms

In the following, a finite element is defined over a polyhedron T by the triplet (T, P, Σ), wherein P is the function
space of the finite element and Σ are the associated DOF,
that is, the dual space of P. The presentation covers some
preliminary definitions of bases for polynomial-valued
differential forms of arbitrary degree r, then introduces
the Lagrange family of elements, and finally extends the
recipe for computation of basis forms to the finite element
families r Λk and r− Λk of polynomial forms on the
n-simplex, which discretize the Hilbert spaces HΛk . An
exemplary depiction of the basis forms for these elements
is shown in Figure 13. A more comprehensive summary of
these elements is given in the Periodic Table of the Finite
Elements (Arnold and Logg, 2014), which is accessible on
the Internet at femtable.org. It is remarked at this point
that closed-form expressions for the basis forms have been
derived in Arnold et al. (2009), which are straightforwardly
amenable to implementation; however, to preserve some
flexibility and to provide insight, the direct construction via
the DOF is outlined. From an implementation standpoint,
it is remarked that it is useful to build upon an abstract
semisymbolic programmatic definition of polynomials,
which can be realized in a simple manner using dictionary
data structures and operator overloading.

with the basis of 1 Λ2 (Δ3 ). The latter example can be
transferred analogously to the construction of finite element
basis forms for r− Λk .
It can be expected that the methodology of the finite
element exterior calculus will increasingly be applied to
engineering problems, despite the mathematical complexity
inherent to the method. A preliminary implementation of
the element families is available in the Fenics project, while
other research groups are actively working on an efficient
implementation. Of course, further research is needed on the
translation of engineering problems to the language of the
exterior calculus.

5 A POSTERIORI ERROR ESTIMATION
AND ADAPTIVITY IN LINEARIZED
ELASTICITY
In the preceding sections, the variational settings in finite and
linearized elasticity were derived. Furthermore, we briefly
discussed some numerical methods, namely FEM, XFEM,
RPKM, and finally the exterior calculus, which are suitable
for obtaining numerical solutions to the related BVPs. A
question that naturally arises concerns the control of errors
obtained by discretizing the variational form of the model

35

problem using the numerical methods mentioned above. This
section therefore commences with error residual equations
and a priori error estimates for an energy-norm control of
the discretization error in linearized elasticity, followed by a
brief overview of energy-norm a posteriori error estimators.
For error estimators applied to the finite elasticity problem,
we refer to Section 7. Later in this section, it will be shown
that these a priori and a posteriori error estimators prove
useful in deriving more general goal-oriented error estimators, where we are mainly interested in estimating the error
of a given so-called goal quantity of engineering interest that
can be any arbitrary bounded linear or nonlinear functional.
Lastly, some remarks about adaptive mesh-refinement strategies and an illustrative numerical example are presented.

5.1

Error residual equation and a priori error
estimates for the finite element method

In this section, we establish error residual equations for the
FEM and show how the discretization error can be estimated
a priori, that is, without knowledge of the finite element
solution, depending on the characteristic element length h
and the polynomial degree p of the shape functions.

5.1.1

Galerkin orthogonality and error residual
equation

As a starting point for the derivation of both a priori and a
posteriori error estimates in linearized elasticity, we introduce the error residual equation based on the weak form of
the residual (108). With the finite element solution uh at hand,
the weak form of the residual vanishes for all vh ∈ h , that
is,
Ru (uh ; vh ) = a(u, vh ) − a(uh , vh ) = 0

∀vh ∈ h

(230)

where we made use of the variational problem (94). This
important property is also referred to as the Galerkin orthogonality and shows that the discretization error
 ∋ e u = u − uh

(231)

is orthogonal to the test functions vh ∈ h with respect to the
bilinear form a. We further conclude that the error eu is the
exact solution to the continuous error residual equation
a(eu , v) = Ru (v)

∀v ∈ 

(232)

which relates the error eu to the residual Ru . Note that this
equation is also referred to as an exact error representation
formula. For brevity, the dependence of the weak residual on
uh is henceforth omitted in the nomenclature.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

In general, the error eu cannot be determined explicitly
from the error residual equation (232), because (232) is a
continuous variational problem (and because the Galerkin
orthogonality (230) forbids to find an approximation of eu in
h ). In a posteriori error analysis, a common strategy for the
construction of an error estimator is therefore to set v = eu ,
which is possible since v is arbitrary and since eu is also in
, to naturally introduce an error measure, namely the norm
induced by the bilinear form a, that is, the energy norm (93),
and, at the same time, to show its relation to the residual as
follows
|||eu |||2 = a(eu , eu ) = Ru (eu )
(233)
For the derivation of certain a posteriori error estimators,
it proves convenient to introduce local versions of the error
residual equation (232), which consist in seeking the local
error contributions eu |Ω in the local spaces e = {v|Ω ∶ v ∈
e
e
} that satisfy
ae (eu |Ω , ve ) = Ru,e (ve )
e

∀ve ∈ e

|||eu ||| ≤ |||u − 𝜋h u|||

|||eu ||| ≤ Chp |u|H p+1 (Ω)

A priori error estimates in the energy norm
and in the H 1 -norm

In order to derive a priori error estimates, we first combine
the -ellipticity with the continuity of the bilinear form a,
which results in
||eu ||H 1 (Ω) ≤ C inf ||u − vh ||H 1 (Ω)
vh ∈h

(236)

with constant C = M∕𝛼 independent of h, where 𝛼 and M
are the ellipticity and continuity constants, respectively. This
a priori estimate is also referred to as the Céa lemma and
shows that the Galerkin approximation uh is quasi optimal
in the H 1 -norm, that is, ||uh ||H 1 (Ω) is proportional to the best
it can be using the finite-dimensional subspace h . Note that
in the special case where the bilinear form a is symmetric
(as observed in
√ linearized elasticity), we obtain the smaller
constant C = (M∕𝛼).
As mentioned above, rather than using the H 1 -norm the
natural choice to measure the error with respect to (233) is
the energy norm (93). Note that the energy norm is equivalent
to the H 1 -norm. Then, the Céa lemma states that
|||eu ||| ≤ inf |||u − vh |||
vh ∈h

(237)

(239)

where | ⋅ |H p+1 (Ω) is the H p+1 -semi norm and C denotes a
generic constant independent of h. Likewise, in the case of
the H 1 -norm, we get
||eu ||H 1 (Ω) ≤ Chp |u|H p+1 (Ω)

(240)

see Ciarlet (1991). Thus, the finite element solution uh
converges to the exact solution u for both the energy norm
and the H 1 -norm with order (hp ).

The general strategy for a posteriori error
estimation

In this chapter, we focus our attention on deriving
upper-bound error estimators, that is, we seek a computable
quantity 𝜂u , the error estimator, such that
|||eu ||| ≤ C𝜂u

(241)

holds for a constant C. In the best case, the constant C is
equal to one or at least computable. In these cases, the error
estimator is called constant free.
The upper-bound property has the advantage that it is
known that the error eu measured in the energy norm cannot
take larger values than C𝜂u . Moreover, in this manner a
stopping criterion is provided for adaptive mesh refinements.
However, adaptive mesh-refinement schemes, as presented in
Section 5.9, require local information about the error eu and
thus we will also show how the error estimators presented can
be calculated in terms of so-called local error indicators 𝜂u,e
on the element level, for which we have the following relation
{

∑

}1

in terms of local error indicators 𝜂u,e lies in the fact that

the calculation of 𝜂u should be less expensive than the
computation of the finite element solution uh , because
otherwise the computational efforts could be better spent for
the computation of a more accurate finite element solution.
The quality of an error estimator is typically judged in
terms of the effectivity index

Energy-norm a posteriori error estimators
for FEM

The a posteriori error estimators available in linearized
elasticity and other, primarily linear, model problems can be
divided into separate classes. Over the years it emerged and is
now widely accepted that four such classes can be declared,
namely the broad class of residual-type error estimators and
the smaller classes of hierarchical-type error estimators,
gradient averaging-type error estimators, and constitutive
equation error estimators. Subclasses such as explicit and
implicit residual-type error estimators can be defined in
order to give the classification a more concise structure. In
the following sections, we will briefly derive a posteriori
error estimators for each of the classes mentioned above.

5.2.1

Explicit residual-type error estimators based
on a local-to-global approach

We will begin our study of energy-norm a posteriori error
estimators with the class of residual-type error estimators. As
is known, for example from explicit and implicit functions,
the notion of an explicit residual-type error estimator implies
that the error estimator is expressed directly in terms of the
weak form of the residual, whereas an implicit residual-type
error estimator relies indirectly on the weak form of the
residual, because usually an auxiliary problem has to be
solved. In this section, we are exclusively concerned with
explicit residual-type error estimators. This archetype error
estimator is originally due to Babuška and Rheinboldt (1979)
and Babuška and Miller (1987).
The general strategy of deriving an explicit residual-type
error estimator is to make explicit use of the residual Ru , split
into element contributions for computational convenience,
which, after some algebraic manipulations, eventually results
in an estimate of the following general form
Ru (eu ) ≤ C|||eu |||𝜂u

Here, C is an unknown generic constant and 𝜂u is a
computable error estimator of the form (242) that will
be determined later in this section. Combining (244) with
the error residual equation (233) then yields the a posteriori
error estimator
|||eu ||| ≤ C𝜂u

(245)

(243)

which relates the estimated error to the true error. Obviously,
upper-bound error estimators have the property that 𝜃 > 1.
The closer the value is to the desirable value of one, the
sharper and the practically more useful the error estimator is.

for the error measured in the energy norm.

(245) we thus obtain a computable upper error bound on
the energy norm of the true error eu . Since the constant C
is generally unknown, it is customary but not justified to set
C = 1, which typically results in a considerable overestimation of the error. To cope with this problem, some efforts
have been applied to estimate the constant C in (245), see,
for example, Johnson and Hansbo (1992) and Carstensen
and Funken (2000). In the subsequent section, we present a
novel approach where the constant C can be determined and
thus makes the error estimator constant free. To complete the
picture, it should be noted that Verfürth (1994) has shown
how lower bounds on the energy norm of the discretization
error eu can be derived by applying bubble functions on the
element level.

5.2.2

Explicit residual-type error estimators based
on a local-to-local approach

The calculation of the error estimator (245) is computationally inexpensive and easy to implement into existing
finite element programs, also because no auxiliary problems have to be solved as is the case for implicit error
estimators presented in the subsequent sections. However, as
mentioned in the previous section, the upper bound consists
of an unknown constant C, which inevitably appears within
the mathematical derivation of the estimator and turns out to
be always unknown. This is seemingly a serious drawback
of the error estimator, which neutralizes its practically
appealing features and has eventually made it unfavorable in

39

engineering practice. Therefore, we follow Gerasimov et al.
(2015) and present an explicit residual-type error estimator,
which has the virtue that it consists of four computable
constants based on the regularity theory by Kondratiev
(1967). We remark that the derivation presented is restricted
to triangular elements with linear shape functions and
without strong distortions, but it is generally possible to
derive the constants for higher polynomial orders (which
requires an additional constant) and for quadrilateral or even
tetrahedral elements.
Since the error estimator presented in this section is a modified version of the one presented in the preceding section,
the general idea of deriving the error estimator is the same
as presented in (244) and (245). However, the error estimator will be constructed by establishing local bounds on the
(local) weak form of the residual Ru,e rather than using only
the global bound (244). This is to avoid the inequality (254),
which introduced the constant into the upper error bound.
Therefore, we first introduce the local forms of the residual
Ru,e ∶ e → ℝ for which

The Kondratiev regularity theory, named after (Kondratiev,
1967), with application to 2D problems of linearized elasticity enables the derivation of the related computable
local constants CP , CT , CK , and CC . These constants take
different values depending on whether the element Ωe is in
the vicinity of a singular point of the boundary 𝜕Ω (Type
I) or not (Type II). The indices of the constants indicate
from which inequality the constants originate, namely CP
from a Poincaré-type inequality, CT from the scaled-trace
inequality, CK from Korn’s second inequality (264), and
CC from the local coercivity inequality (265). Note that the
Poincaré-type inequality and the scaled-trace inequality are
not shown here, but they are both used to derive the local
interpolation estimates (264). The values of the constants for
linear triangular elements are summarized in Table 1. For
further details on the derivation of the constants, including
all relevant mathematical finesses, we refer to Gerasimov
et al. (2015).

5.2.3

Implicit residual-type error estimators based
on local Neumann problems

Within this strategy, the weak form of the residual is used
locally to solve auxiliary variational problems, the solutions
of which can be used to derive an error estimator. The local
problems can be either pure Neumann or pure Dirichlet problems. The subject of this section is the derivation of the
former case, that is, implicit a posteriori error estimators
based on the solutions of local Neumann problems subjected
to equilibrated residuals, as introduced by Bank and Weiser
(1985) and Ainsworth and Oden (1993). For the construction of the equilibrated residuals, we refer to Ladevèze and
Leguillon (1983). Error estimators of the local Dirichlet type
are treated in the subsequent section.

As we have seen in the preceding sections, a key issue of
all a posteriori error estimators available is to compute error
indicators on the element level such that the sum of these
local contributions yields the error estimator in the sense of
(242). A natural idea is therefore to begin the construction of
the error estimator with the local form of the error residual
equation (234). However, as we will see later in this section,
the local problem (234) has the disadvantage that the local
residual Ru,e is not computable and therefore needs to be
replaced by a computable approximation R̃ u,e , which then
results in the modified local problem of searching for a
(modified) solution 𝝍 u,e ∈ e as an approximation of eu |Ω

introduced in Section 5.2.1. As can be observed, we obtain
large displacements where the error is large, namely at the
bottom corners of the square plate, and small displacements
and thus small errors at the top corners.
