as the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality is the only inequality
used to derive the estimator. It needs to be emphasized,
however, that this bound is only guaranteed for functions
belonging to the infinite-dimensional space e . Thus, in a
finite element code higher order shape functions are implemented to retain the upper-bound property of the estimator.
For intrinsically computable upper error bounds, we refer the
interested reader to Babuška et al. (1999).

basic idea of equilibrating and thus improving the approximate tractions can be traced back to Bufler and Stein (1970)

5.2.4

Implicit residual-type error estimators based
on local Dirichlet problems

In the classical works by Babuška and Rheinboldt (1978a,
1978b) an implicit residual-type error estimator was derived
that relies on the solution of local Dirichlet problems.
Evidently, the finite element approximation of Dirichlet
problems has the significant advantage over Neumann
problems that they are a priori well posed in terms of the
imposed boundary conditions, since in this case the Dirichlet
boundary conditions are already fulfilled by the choice of
the finite element subspace.
The basic strategy of the error estimator is briefly outlined
as follows: As a point of departure, we again consider the
error residual equation (232). Making use of the condition
that the (globally extended) finite element ansatz function
associated with a node Xn ∈ Ω, that is, vn |Ω = Nv v̂ e , is

constructed in such a manner that it takes the value one at
the node, whereas it vanishes at the remaining nodes, it is
easily verified that the error residual equation (232) may be
recast as

Gradient averaging-type error estimators

In this section, we are concerned with the so-called
gradient averaging-type error estimators as introduced
by Zienkiewicz and Zhu (1987). Such error estimators rely
on the obvious fact that the gradients, that is, the strain
and stress fields, as obtained by the finite element approximation uh are generally discontinuous on the interelement
boundaries 𝜕Ωe and therefore visualize the finite element
discretization error eu , since the exact strain and stress

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

preferably simple postprocessing technique. This can be

were developed in recent years. The most common strategies

Brauchli and Oden (1971), and the popular so-called superconvergent patch recovery (SPR) technique as introduced
by Zienkiewicz and Zhu (1992c).
The SPR technique relies on the fact that point values
of approximate stresses are more accurate in the element
domain Ωe than on the boundaries 𝜕Ωe . Under certain
conditions, the so-called sampling points exhibit even superconvergence properties. Thus, it sounds reasonable to project

Constitutive equation error estimators

Another class of error estimators is based on the error in the
constitutive equation (102b) and was introduced for linear
problems by Ladevèze and Leguillon (1983) and developed
further mainly by the same group, see, for example, Ladevèze
and Rougeot (1997). At first sight, this seems confusing
as the constitutive equation (102b) is fulfilled a priori for
the finite element solution uh in a strong sense. To gain
a better understanding of this class of error estimators, let
us therefore first recall the weak and strong forms of the
linearized elasticity problem as stated in (94) and (102),
respectively. By construction of the weak form (94) it is
obvious that only the equilibrium equation (102a) is fulfilled
by the finite element solution uh in a weak sense, whereas
the constitutive equation (102b) and the kinematic equation
(102c) are fulfilled in a strong sense.
The constitutive equation error estimator is based
on the following idea: we assume that there exists a
displacement–stress pair (ukin , 𝝈 eq ) ∈  ×  with  as
defined in (83) (with r = 2) and  as defined in (165).
In other words, the displacements ukin are required to be
kinematically admissible in the sense that they fulfill the
kinematic equation (102c) and the Dirichlet boundary conditions (103a). Thus, it is sufficient that ukin ∈ . The stresses
𝝈 eq are required to be statically admissible in the sense that
they fulfill the equilibrium equation (102a) and the Neumann
boundary conditions (103b) in the weak sense (94).
Substituting the pair (ukin , 𝝈 eq ) into the constitutive
equation (102b) then results in the following error
 ∋ econ = 𝝈 eq − C ∶ 𝜺(ukin )

(315)

Note that by definition of the constitutive equation (71), the
error econ ∈  has the unit of a stress. Evidently, the error
econ vanishes if and only if the pair (ukin , 𝝈 eq ) is the exact
solution, that is, ukin = u and 𝝈 eq = C ∶ 𝜺(u).
By construction of the FEM, its solution uh ∈ h ⊂  is
obviously one possibility to choose a kinematically admissible field ukin . It becomes also clear, however, that the finite
element stress field 𝝈(uh ) cannot be chosen as a statically
admissible stress field, since it does not fulfill the weak
form (94). Indeed, the construction of a statically admissible stress field 𝝈 eq is cumbersome and cannot generally be
fulfilled exactly. For further details on different techniques
for obtaining approximate 𝝈 eq in terms of a postprocessing of

con |Ωe |||𝜎,Ωe . We remark that similar to
the implicit residual-type a posteriori error estimator as
presented in Section 5.2.3, the upper-bound property of the
error estimator 𝜂u is guaranteed only if the computable stress
field 𝝈 eq fulfills (318) exactly. To cope with this problem,
𝝈 eq is usually determined in higher order local spaces, which
results in an upper error bound in most computations. If, on
the other hand, the computable stress field 𝝈 eq is close to
the true stress field 𝝈(u) in the sense of the complementary
energy norm, then |||𝝈(u) − 𝝈 eq |||𝜎 becomes small and as a
consequence the error estimator 𝜂u provides a sharp bound.
As mentioned above, the constitutive error estimator
(319) bears resemblance to the implicit residual-type error
estimator (272) not only in terms of the constant-free error
bounds but also in an implicit strategy to obtain those, which
relies on the solutions of auxiliary Neumann problems on
the element level. However, the implicit residual-type error
estimator (272) is based on the error residual equation (232),
whereas the constitutive equation error estimator (319) is
based on a recovered stress field that is then measured in
the complementary energy norm. Thus, (319) bears also
resemblance to gradient averaging-type error estimators as
presented in the preceding section with the advantage that
upper error bounds can be achieved in this case.

5.3

Energy-norm a posteriori error estimators
for XFEM

In a similar way as the XFEM, as introduced in Section 3.4,
is an extension of the FEM, the associated a posteriori
error estimators can be derived, in most cases, as an extension of the error estimators for the FEM as elaborated in
the preceding section. However, the elements that contain a
crack and especially the crack tip element, or, more precisely,
every element that is enriched, need a special treatment.
Thus, the well-established error estimators developed for
FEM need a proper extension or at least a modification to be
applicable to XFEM approximations.
Gradient averaging-type error estimators are the ones with
the lowest efforts in implementation and therefore they are
a popular choice also in XFEM. For example, Bordas and
Duflot (2007) introduced a recovery procedure where a
MLS basis is extended by crack-tip asymptotic functions
in the entire domain, which has the vital drawback that this
approach is quite costly. A remedy to overcome this issue is
proposed by the same authors in Duflot and Bordas (2008)

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

and uses finite element shape functions that are enriched in
the vicinity of the crack tip. In the energy- norm approach
presented by Ródenas et al. (2008), the stress field around
the crack tip is split additively into singular and regular
parts each of which is recovered separately by an adaptation
of the well-known SPR technique. Recently, Prange et al.
(2012) made use of a least-square fit problem to obtain the
recovered gradient field used for the error estimator.
Residual-type error estimators have also been developed
for XFEM. Ródenas et al. (2010) presented an implicit
residual-type approach where recovered gradients are
constructed that are equilibrated and thus can be used to
derive upper bounds on the XFEM discretization error
measured in the energy norm. The explicit residual-type a
posteriori error estimator presented in Section 5.2.1 was
recently extended to XFEM approximations by Hild et al.
(2011) for P1 -triangular elements and by Gerasimov et al.
(2012) for Q1 quadrilaterals, which are frequently used in
XFEM. The latter explicit residual-type error estimator will
be discussed in greater detail in the following section.

5.4

Energy-norm a posteriori error estimators
for RKPM

From an error control point of view, Galerkin-type meshfree methods such as RKPM have the advantage that error
estimation techniques, as extensively developed for FEM,
see Section 5.2, can generally be transferred to meshfree
methods. However, the development of error estimators for
meshfree methods is still in an early stage.
While the basic principle of gradient averaging-type error
estimators, as discussed in Section 5.2.6, can be applied to
Galerkin-type meshfree methods as well, the recovery techniques usually need to be redeveloped. This is due to the
fact that the meshfree gradient approximations are already
smooth in most cases, whereas the ones obtained from
FEM are usually discontinuous on the interelement boundaries as mentioned above. Chung and Belytschko (1998)
derived a recovery technique for meshfree methods in terms
of projected gradients obtained by shape functions with a
smaller support size that are multiplied by the values of the
gradients at the particles. The estimator derived by Gavete
et al. (2003) relies on two postprocessing approaches of the
Galerkin solution where two MLS approximations are used
with different support sizes but same weighting functions.
More recently, Lee and Shuai (2007) introduced another
recovery procedure based on an extraction operator applied
to the approximate gradient field as obtained by the meshfree
method.
Mathematically more sound error estimators with error
bounds can be found in the class of residual-type error estimators. An explicit residual-type error estimator similar to
the one presented in Section 5.2.1 was developed by Duarte
and Oden (1996a) for the meshfree method by the same
authors, called h-p clouds, see Duarte and Oden (1996b).
They take advantage of the fact that no interior boundary
residuals appear in meshfree methods. More recently, Vidal
et al. (2008) presented an implicit residual-type error estimator, where the auxiliary local problems are solved on
patches of the integration cells so that no fluxes need to be
taken into account. Their flux-free implicit-type error estimator can be seen as an extension of the finite element
counterpart as introduced by Parés et al. (2006) to meshfree
methods.
Here, we follow Rüter and Chen (2015) to derive an
energy-norm error estimator of implicit residual type, which
is based on a projected error residual equation to account
for Nitsche’s method and to get a symmetric form. The
error estimator is established in terms of local forms of the
projected error residual equation on each (Gauss) integration cell.

5.4.1

The error residual equation

The discretization error associated with the (discrete) RKPM
solution uh is defined in the usual way as in (231). In
Section 5.1.1, the error residual equation (232) was derived
for the finite element discretization error. In general, this
error residual equation also holds for the case of meshfree
RKPM approximations. However, in (232), the test functions
v are restricted to the test space , whereas the discretization
error eu is now in the space
{
}
E = v ∈ [H 1 (Ω)]3 ; v|ΓD = e|ΓD

(331)

which, in turn, is a subset of [H 1 (Ω)]3 rather than , because
of the missing Kronecker delta property of the meshfree
shape functions as explained in Section 3.5.
To cope with this problem, we extend the error residual
equation (232) to the case where v can be chosen from
[H 1 (Ω)]3 . The extended form of (232) is based on integration by parts of the equilibrium equation (102a) and on the
assumption that v does not vanish on ΓD . It then follows
that in the extended error residual equation the discretization
error eu ∈ E satisfies

The above residual R̂ u is extended in the sense that it
emanates from Ru by adding the residual on the Dirichlet
boundary. For this reason, the Dirichlet boundary ΓD turns
into a Neumann-type boundary with prescribed tractions
from the meshfree Galerkin solution uh . The solution of the
weak form is then still the error eu , but additional contributions from ΓD are taken into account. Obviously, if v is chosen
from  ⊂ [H 1 (Ω)]3 , then (332) simplifies to the well-known
form (232). Moreover, in this sense, (332) is an extension of
(232).
The well posedness of the extended error residual equation
(332) is difficult to show, since the left-hand side is not
even symmetric. Even in the case of symmetrization,
coercivity remains an issue. Therefore, and also to deal
with the bilinear form a only, we propose to introduce a

5.4.2

Implicit residual-type error estimators based
on local Neumann problems

The general procedure of deriving an implicit residual-type
error estimator for RKPM discretizations is similar to the
procedure for FEM as outlined in Section 5.2.3. However,
rather than starting from the local error residual equation
for finite element discretizations (234), we now begin our
derivations from the local projected error residual equation
(336), targeted to meshfree methods. Since the tractions at
the integration cell boundaries are again generally unknown,
the extended weak residual R̂ u is not computable in (336) and
is therefore again replaced by a computable approximation
R̃ u,i , which yields the modified local problem

and thus satisfies

holds. As a consequence, the computable tractions ̃ti,h need
to fulfill the compatibility condition ̃ti,h = −̃ta,h on Ωi ∩ Ωa
for each boundary between two adjacent integration cells Ωi
and Ωa . Note that this requirement is automatically fulfilled
by the tractions obtained from the RKPM approximation
uh owing to the high regularity of the discrete solution.
Furthermore, the tractions ̃ti,h need to satisfy the Neumann
boundary condition (103b), that is, ̃ti,h = t in Γf ⊂ ΓN as
well as the discrete Neumann boundary condition ̃ti,h = th in
Γf ⊂ ΓD .
The equilibration procedure for the RKPM tractions is
thus generally easier than for finite elements, because the
boundary conditions are trivial to fulfill and thus the RKPM
tractions only need equilibration whereas the finite element
tractions also need to be forced to fulfill the compatibility
condition between adjacent elements. In general, an equilibration technique similar to the one used for the FEM can be
used to determine the equilibrated tractions ̃ti,h .

5.5

Goal-oriented a priori error estimates
and strategies for a posteriori error estimation

We now turn our attention to the error control of goaloriented error measures, since they are usually of greater
interest to the practical engineer than an energy-norm
control of the discretization error. Moreover, as it turns
out, energy-norm error estimators, as presented in the
preceding section, can be envisioned as a special case of
goal-oriented estimators where the error measure is defined
as the energy norm.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

52

Goal-oriented error estimators rely on the solution of a dual
problem. Therefore, we also introduce duality techniques in
order to be able to generalize the error measure from the
energy norm to other norms or, more generally, to arbitrary
goal-oriented error measures. The corresponding a priori
estimates based on duality techniques are closely related to
the Aubin–Nitsche lemma. With the primal and the dual finite
element solutions at hand, we are then able to devise a general
scheme for goal-oriented a posteriori error estimation.

5.5.1

Error measures

Since the secant form ES involves the exact solution u,
we next introduce an approximation ET ∶  → ℝ of ES by
replacing the exact solution u with the approximate solution
uh . Thus, we obtain the tangent form
ET (⋅) = E′ (uh , uh ; ⋅) = ES (uh , uh ; ⋅) ≈ ES (u, uh ; ⋅)

possibilities out of many to measure the error eu . In the most
general case, the appropriate goal-oriented error measure is
given by the nonlinear functional E ∶  ×  → ℝ that can be
any Gâteaux differentiable (non)linear functional satisfying
the condition E(v, v) = 0 for all v ∈ , where we adopted
the terminology introduced by Larsson et al. (2002). Hence,
E may be the energy (or any other) norm of the discretization error eu as a special case. Upon introducing the generally nonlinear goal quantity of interest Q ∶  → ℝ, we may
define the error measure E either as the quantity of the error
E(u, uh ) = Q(u − uh ) = Q(eu )

(353)

or as the error of the quantity

Duality techniques and error representations

The a priori and a posteriori estimation of the goal-oriented
error measure E requires the solution of an auxiliary dual
problem. Therefore, we will discuss below duality techniques in greater detail.
To begin with, we introduce the dual bilinear form a∗ ∶
 ×  → ℝ of a given by
a∗ (w, v) = a(v, w) ∀v, w ∈ 

(354)

Note that in the special case where the goal quantity of
interest Q is a linear functional, it is a simple observation that
the quantity of the error and the error of the quantity coincide.
With the fundamental theorem of calculus, we may introduce
the relation
E(u, uh ) = E(u, uh ) − E(uh , uh )

A priori error estimates in the L2 -norm

With the duality technique presented, we can now derive
an a priori error estimate of the discretization error eu in a
norm other than the energy norm or the H 1 -norm, which is
well known as the Aubin–Nitsche lemma, see, for example,
Braess (2007). To begin with, we introduce the Hilbert space
. With the dual norm, we recognize that

(368)

Existence and uniqueness of the variational problems
presented above is assured if the bilinear form a∗ as well as
the linear functionals ES , ET , Ru∗ , and Ru∗ satisfy the assumptions of the Lax–Milgram theorem. Now it becomes obvious
that the error measure E and thus the linear functional ES
and its approximation ET are required to be bounded functionals. If, however, the functional is unbounded, a remedy
is found using a suitable regularization of the unbounded
functional.
It is worth noticing that alternatively, the dual problem
(360) may be derived using the property that Hilbert spaces
are reflexive spaces. This approach has been followed by
Prudhomme and Oden (1999).
With the exact and approximate dual solutions at hand,
we may now derive error representation formulas for the
goal-oriented error measure E that serve as a basis for every
goal-oriented a priori and a posteriori error estimate within
the theory of linearized elasticity. Recall that at this stage,
we have at our disposal the exact error residual equation
of the primal problem (232) and its dual counterparts (365)
and (368). All that remains is to show that there exists a
link between them. This link is simply achieved by setting
again v = eu in the dual problem (360). Hence, with (357)
this results in the exact error representation
E(u, uh ) = a∗ (u − 𝜋h u, eu )

goal-oriented error estimator 𝜂u,u∗ is to use the product form
𝜂u,u∗ = 𝜂u 𝜂u∗

Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we are able to resort to
energy-norm a posteriori error estimators for both the
primal and the dual problems.

holds. Now it becomes clear that if the angle 𝜑 is small,
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality does not yield a large overestimation of the error measure E. This situation changes
drastically if the cosine of 𝜑 becomes small.
To summarize the strategies (382), (383), (384), and (385)
presented above, it needs to be emphasized that upper error

bounds on the error measure E can be obtained, but the
“more guaranteed” they are, the less sharp they become. Note
also that the estimators (384) and (385), which are based on
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, are restricted to self-adjoint
problems, whereas (382) and (383) are more versatile.

5.6

Goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators
for FEM

Although with the general strategy as presented in
Section 5.5.4 every energy-norm error estimator for the
FEM, as introduced in Section 5.2, can be turned into a
goal-oriented error estimator, we focus our attention in the
following sections on three principally different strategies
of goal-oriented a posteriori error estimation to cover the
classes of residual-type, hierarchical-type, and gradient
averaging-type error estimators. In this section, we restrict
our considerations to error estimators for the finite element
discretization error. Goal-oriented error estimators for
XFEM and RKPM will be derived in Sections 5.7 and 5.8,
respectively.

5.6.1

Implicit residual-type error estimators based
on local Neumann problems

In the first strategy, we apply the well-known approach of
solving, in turn, local Neumann problems for both the primal
and the dual problems. The general procedure has already
been explained by means of the primal problem – as used to
derive energy-norm error estimators – in Section 5.2.3 and
is virtually the same for the derivation of energy-norm error
estimators for the dual problem. Hence, upon recalling the
local Neumann problem for the primal problem (269), we
may similarly establish the local problem associated with the
(approximate) error residual equation of the dual problem

5.6.2

Hierarchical-type error estimators based
on local Dirichlet problems

We now resort to the basic idea of hierarchical error estimators as presented in Section 5.2.5, where we have seen
that a hierarchical error estimator relies on the computation
of a better approximation than the finite element solution in
terms of the saturation assumption. Hence, an approximation of the error could be computed, which then may serve to
compute error estimators as presented in the general strategy
in Section 5.5.4. As it turns out, however, such a strategy

used technique to control the finite element discretization error in the energy norm is based on averaging
techniques, or, more precisely, on recovery of the approximate stress field 𝝈(uh ). Therefore, the objective of this
section is to discuss how a recovery technique can
be applied to estimate the goal-oriented error measure
E.

5.7

Goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators
for XFEM

In this section, we deal with extensions of the goal-oriented
a posteriori error estimators as presented for the FEM in
the preceding section to extended finite element approximations as introduced in Section 3.4, see also Extended Finite
Element Methods.
The first steps toward goal-oriented error estimators in
XFEM have been carried out by Panetier et al. (2010) and
Rüter and Stein (2011). In Panetier et al. (2010), an extension
of the constitutive equation error estimation strategy as introduced in Section 5.2.7 is presented, whereas in Rüter and
Stein (2011), the classical implicit residual-type error estimator for the FEM, as derived in Section 5.2.3, is applied to
estimate goal-oriented error measures resulting from XFEM
discretizations. For the sake of conciseness, we focus our
attention in this section on the derivation of an explicit
residual-type goal-oriented error estimator, as introduced in
Rüter et al. (2013), that can be seen as an extension to the
one presented in Section 5.3.1 for an energy-norm control of
the discretization error eu .

5.7.1

Explicit residual-type error estimators

The explicit residual-type goal-oriented error estimator
presented in Rüter et al. (2013) is based on the general estimator (386). Although, as mentioned earlier, this estimator
is assumed to be the least sharp of the estimators presented
in Section 5.5.4, this has virtually no effect on the error
estimator presented in this section, because the constant C
in the error estimator remains unknown.
The strategy is therefore to introduce an energy-norm
explicit residual-type error estimator for the discretization
error of the dual problem eu∗ in much the same way as
presented in Sections 5.2.1 and 5.3.1 for the case of the
primal problem.
As in Sections 5.2.1 and 5.3.1, we begin our derivations
with the error residual equation, in this case with (368), that
is, the (approximate) error residual equation associated with
the dual problem. Setting v = eu∗ in (368), we obtain
|||eu∗ |||2 = a(eu∗ , eu∗ ) = Ru (eu∗ )

5.8

Goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators
for RKPM

As mentioned in Section 5.4, the development of error
estimators for RKPM discretizations, as presented in
Section 3.5, is still in its early stage. This holds especially
true for goal-oriented error estimators. To date, only two
such error estimators have been developed. Both can be
classified as implicit residual-type error estimators. The first
one was presented by Vidal et al. (2008) as an extension
of their energy-norm error estimator based on flux-free
local problems. The second one, which will be derived in
this section, was recently introduced by Rüter and Chen
(2015) and also relies on their energy-norm error estimator
as discussed in Section 5.4.
A clear advantage of using goal-oriented error estimators
for meshfree methods is the independence of the particles
from the integration cells Ωi , which makes it possible to use

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

60

different discretizations for the primal and the dual problems
at no additional cost. This multispace approach thus bypasses
the transfer of discrete solutions from one mesh to the other
as is required for the FEM, as shown in Rüter et al. (2007).
It is therefore tailored to meshfree methods and adds versatility to the goal-oriented error estimator presented in this
section.

5.8.1

Duality techniques based on a multispace

where a∗h = ah as defined in (159), since the underlying

To estimate the error of the approximate error measure ET ,
the general strategy is again to set v = eu in the dual problem
(366). Note, however, that the test functions v are required to
be in the space , whereas eu is an element of E as defined in
(331). As with the derivation of the error residual equation in

(366) to the case where v can be chosen from [H 1 (Ω)]3 , which
results in
∗

61

(414)

Note that the last two terms on the right-hand side in (414)
are exactly computable and thus only the first three terms
need to be estimated. We further remark that the last two
terms on the right-hand side of (414) appear, because the
classical Galerkin orthogonality relation is violated. This
has two reasons: first, the discretization error eu is not in
the same space as the exact solution u. Second, the use
of different finite-dimensional subspaces h and h∗ for the
discrete primal and dual problems, respectively, also leads to
a violation of the Galerkin orthogonality.
The obvious advantage of using different finitedimensional subspaces h and h∗ is that it does not require
any additional computational efforts as it would be the
case for FEM. This is because, for example, the (extended)
residual of the primal problem R needs to be computed in
∗
terms of the RKPM solution of the dual problem uh , which
∗
requires to integrate the dual RKPM solution uh using the
integration cells Ωi of the primal problem. This situation
is depicted in Figures 22 and 23, where the primal and a
different (in this case coarser) dual discretizations are plotted
using the same integration cells from the primal problem. In
a similar way, if the error measure E is nonlinear, then, as we
have seen in (358), its tangent form depends on the discrete
primal solution uh , which is then used in the discrete dual
problem (412) and thus using the integration cells Ωi of the
dual problem. However, since the particles are independent
from the mesh in a meshfree method, these computations
are ‘a piece of cake’, as opposed to a mesh-based method,
where the transfer of the solution from one mesh to the

Adaptive refinement techniques

Now that we have at our disposal various energy-norm and
goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators, the questions
naturally arise where and how to refine the finite element
mesh and the particles in a meshfree method in order to
reduce and equidistribute the discretization error. The answer
to the first question is fairly simple. A common strategy to
find the elements (or the particles in the integration cells
in a meshfree method) that are going to be refined reads as
follows: an element (or particles in an integration cell) is (are)
refined if the scaled error indicator on the element level (or in
the integration cell), that is, the error indicator 𝜂u,e divided by
the largest error indicator max 𝜂u,e , exceeds a given tolerance
𝜀, which is usually chosen between 0.5 and 0.8. Note that
alternatively, one may refine a fixed amount of, say, 30% of
the elements (or particles in an integration cell) with largest

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

62

Figure 24. Coarse starting mesh with elements to be refined (dark
gray).

error contributions. This strategy, however, usually leads to
suboptimal error convergence.
Focusing first on the FEM, the elements with large error
contributions need to be refined for which various techniques have been developed over the years. For the sake of
conciseness, we restrict our considerations in this chapter to
mesh-refinement strategies for quadrilateral finite elements.
Furthermore, we briefly present h-adaptivity for triangular,
tetrahedral, and hexahedral finite elements. For mesh generation and remeshing techniques based on error-density functions, we refer the reader to Shephard and Georges (1991)
and Mesh Generation and Mesh Adaptivity: Theory and
Techniques. At the end of this section, we also briefly
present adaptive refinement techniques that can be applied
to meshfree methods.

5.9.1

Node-regular refinement of quadrilateral
elements using transition elements

For node-regular mesh refinements, distorted transition
elements are required. Those elements may clearly vanish in
the next refinement step. Because of the transition elements,
there arises a transition zone between the refined and the
unrefined elements, which increases the influence zone
of a local refinement. An example for node-regular mesh
refinement can be seen in Figure 25, where the elements
in dark gray are adaptively refined starting from the coarse
mesh as depicted in Figure 24. Note that an alternating
black-white coloring is used to keep the band structure of
the global stiffness matrix.

5.9.2

Distortion-free refinement of quadrilateral
elements using hanging nodes

Alternatively, rather than distorting the elements temporarily,
one can use the technique of hanging nodes, where the
elements are not distorted but instead irregular nodes are

63

Figure 25. Node-regular adaptive mesh refinement.

Figure 26. Adaptive mesh refinement using hanging nodes.

required as depicted in Figure 26. Those irregular nodes are
eliminated by proper interpolations according to the trial
functions. This technique has the obvious advantage over
the node-regular refinement technique mentioned previously
that a transition zone between refined and unrefined elements
does not arise.

5.9.3

Refinement techniques for triangular,
tetrahedral, and hexahedral elements

Triangular elements are regularly refined by halving the
longest side or using Delaunay techniques. It is important to
note that small angles should be avoided in the real geometry.
Furthermore, the diagonal structure of the global stiffness
matrix should be retained, see also Finite Element Methods.
Likewise, tetrahedral elements are regularly subdivided
into four sub-tetrahedrals or into two tetrahedrals by halving
the longest side. Again, the angles have to be controlled.
As with quadrilateral elements, hexahedral elements,
as introduced in Section 3.3.1, can be refined by a
node-regular technique or by employing hanging nodes.
Using node-regular mesh refinements, the element faces

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

of the transition elements look like elements in the
two-dimensional case, cf. Section 5.9.1. However, one
has to take into account that inside the transition element
degenerated elements temporarily appear at about 3%
of the element volume by coinciding neighboring sides.
Those degenerated elements have the shape of so-called
triedra or heptahedra, see Niekamp and Stein (2002). It
should be noted that if one is to refine a hexahedral element
into eight sub-hexahedral elements, 84 transition elements
are required, which makes the mesh refinement considerably nonlocal and turns out to be a disadvantage of the
node-regular refinement technique.
A remedy is found using the concept of hanging nodes,
which keeps the refinement local. Furthermore, this approach
is much easier from theoretical and numerical points
of view.

5.9.4

A proposal toward anisotropic hp-adaptivity

Adaptive particle refinements for meshfree
methods

Meshfree methods have a clear advantage over mesh-based
methods concerning h-adaptive refinements of the discretization. Adding, moving, and removing particles is generally
easy, even in the case that a particle-independent background
mesh is used for integration purposes. Therefore, tedious
refinement techniques as presented in the preceding sections
are not required for meshfree methods.

In this numerical example, we are concerned with a
precracked glass plate and we investigate the fracture
behavior of the plate. This is carried out by means of the
J-integral, originally introduced by Cherepanov (1967) and
Rice (1968), as a fracture criterion within the theory of
LEFM, see Computational Fracture Mechanics. Therefore, we are interested in controlling the error obtained while
approximately evaluating the J-integral using the numerical
methods presented in this chapter, that is, FEM, XFEM, and
meshfree RKPM as introduced in Sections 3.3, 3.4, and 3.5,
respectively.

5.10.1

The J-integral and its linearization

First, we introduce the domain expression of the J-integral,
as introduced by Shih et al. (1986), rather than its original
contour expression. In a material force setting within Eshelbian mechanics, the J-integral then takes the form
J(u) = − 𝚺(u) ∶ H(qx) dA
∫ ΩJ

(422)

Here, x is the unit vector in the direction of crack propagation and q = q(x, y) (or q = q(x, y, z) in three space dimensions) represents an arbitrary but continuously differentiable
weighting function with q = 1 at the crack tip and q = 0 on
the contour (or surface) ΓJ that bounds the arbitrary area
(or volume) ΩJ , see Figures 9 and 27. Furthermore, 𝚺 =
w0 I − HT ⋅ 𝝈 denotes the Newton–Eshelby stress tensor. Note
that it was tacitly assumed that the crack faces are traction
free and that body forces are omitted.

F
ΓJ

350mm

5.9.5

Numerical example: cracked plate subjected
to four-point bending

350mm

So far, we confined ourselves to h-adaptivity. As was already
observed in Section 5.5.3, however, p-adaptivity is more efficient, since the error in the energy norm converges with
order (hp ). Therefore, hp-adaptivity can provide exponential convergence, see, for example, Szabó and Babuška
(1991), and might need anisotropic, that is, directional h- and
p-updates. More recently, Houston et al. (2003) presented
Sobolev regularity in order to control hp-adaptivity.
Simple indicators for hp-adaptivity were given by Stein
and Ohnimus (1997) according to the following rule: If the
a posteriori error estimator considered is of the size of the
associated a priori error estimator (with an uncertainty factor
two to three), then p is increased while h remains unchanged.
If the a posteriori error estimator is considerably smaller,
then h is reduced while p remains unchanged.
There is obviously no doubt that hp-adaptivity will play an
important role in the future, especially for large 3D systems.
Furthermore, anisotropic techniques – according to geometrical and material properties – will improve the computational efficiency even more.

We begin our error analysis with finite element discretizations and present numerical results taken from Rüter and
Stein (2006). The system considered in this section is a
parallel-edge-cracked borosilicate glass plate in plane-stress
state subjected to four-point bending, as depicted in
Figure 27. For the chosen borosilicate glass, the following
material data are assumed: E = 64 000 N mm−2 , 𝜈 = 0.2,
and critical value Jc = 0.015 kJ m−2 . The chosen (primal)
load in this example is F = 0.6 N mm−2 . Owing to symmetry
considerations, only one half of the specimen is modeled.
The reference solution for the value of the J-integral is
obtained using Q2 elements with 128 054 DOF within an
adaptive mesh-refinement scheme. The value obtained then
reads J∕2 = 0.0128862 kJ m−2 . The discrete solution uh is
obtained using isoparametric Q1 elements.

introduced in Section 5.9.1, is driven by goal-oriented
residual-type error estimators based on local Neumann
problems, a hierarchical-type error estimator based on local
Dirichlet problems, or gradient averaging-type error estimators based on recovery of the stress fields as presented in
Sections 5.6.1, 5.6.2, and 5.6.3, respectively.

except for “residual 4”, show good convergence behavior.

either the conventional enrichment (or branch) functions
(147) or the new enrichment (or branch) functions (150),
henceforth abbreviated in the figures as “cbf” and “nbf”,
respectively. As can be seen, the rate of convergence of
all error estimators investigated is optimal. The unknown
constant in the explicit error estimators was set here to
one, which results in considerable overestimation in this
example. In general, overestimation can be expected as a
consequence of the various inequalities used in the derivation of the error estimator. Remarkably, the true errors
for Q1 -XFEM and Q2 -FEM are about the same, which
shows the advantage of using crack tip enrichment functions in the XFEM discretizations. Moreover, it should
be pointed out that we find in this example additional
singularities at the points where the boundary conditions
change from Dirichlet to Neumann. These additional strong
singularities are not captured by the XFEM enrichment
functions.

6

DETERMINISTIC VERIFICATION AND
VALIDATION BY COUPLED
DISCRETIZATION AND EXPANSIVE
MODEL ADAPTIVITY

Secured processes for verification and validation of models
and analysis have to yield evidence of their predictive
capability, as the requirement for confidence and decisions,
guaranteeing safe and robust products. Adequate physical,
mathematical, and computational engineering modeling
of complex structures – like wide-spanned bridges and
halls – is a complicated task which requires not only the
mastery of analysis but also engineering expertise in order to
guarantee structural and material safety and reliability for all
relevant actions on the structure. Engineering reality has to
implement probabilistic and stochastic loadings and material
properties. These important influences are represented in
the chapter Predictive Computational Science: Computer
Predictions in the Presence of Uncertainty, as well as
in the chapter Uncertainty Quantification and Bayesian
Inversion.

6.1

Concepts for expansive and reductive model
adaptivity coupled with discretization
adaptivity

In accordance with this engineering thinking, the design of
a complicated structure progresses from coarse global to
more refined local models to detect the leading structural
and material properties that affect load carrying, damage,

instability, and failure, that is, guaranteeing safety and
reliability of complex structures. In this spirit, this chapter
treats goal-oriented error-controlled combined expansive
model and discretization adaptivity. Applications address
theoretical and numerical validation, mainly in structural
engineering practice. The methodology realizes engineering
thinking and acting to proceed from experience of simpler
to more complicated modeling of material and system
properties.
Expansive model adaptivity (with model refinements), as
a part of model validation, is defined as a bottom-up strategy
with an upgrade from a coarse basis model, which is sufficiently accurate in major parts of a system, to more complicated models in relatively small subdomains, depending on
boundary conditions, loading, and deformation processes.
An example is the “expansion” from linear elastic 2 12 D plate
or shell theory to linear elastic 3D continuum theory (dimensional expansion) in boundary layers. Furthermore, the
appearance of inelastic deformations in certain subdomains
(constitutive expansion) or the appearance of load-depending
structural instabilities in certain subdomains (kinematic
expansion) can cause model updates, see Stein et al. (2007).
Model expansion (for validation) has to be coupled with
discretization adaptivity of the coarse model (for verification) in order to control the discretization and model errors
of the coarse model in all subdomains with the same order of
accuracy.
In contrast to model validation by model expansion, model
reduction, as a top-down strategy, starts from a fine model
(here 3D elasticity), which covers all expected mechanical
effects in a sufficiently accurate way. Model reduction aims
at topology, geometry, and load depending model coarsening
in subdomains where a hierarchically simplified theory is
sufficiently accurate, as published by, for example, P. Ciarlet,
C. Schwab, M. Dauge, I. Babuška, and J. Pitkäranta. It is
obvious that model reduction is valuable for basic investigations on the validity of coarse models, especially their errors
in boundary layers and other disturbed subdomains, but not
as an effective tool for engineering adaptivity of specific
structural systems.
It should be mentioned that model reduction is a widely
used strategy for detecting the decisive vibration modes of
a structure, including kinetic influences on stress concentrations.
There are different goals of combined model and
discretization adaptivity in the applications of Oden et al.
and Stein et al. In Oden’s articles, the system of the coarse
model usually is a homogeneous elastic 3D C1 -continuum
and the fine model describes a microstructure, for example,
3D fiber-reinforced epoxy material (each component is also
elastic). A finite element analysis is then applied to the fine
model with subsequent homogenization to get local model

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

68

errors. Dimensional adaptivity does not occur in this process
and therefore, prolongations from the coarse to the fine
model are not included.
In most of Stein’s applications, the mathematical model is
based on a homogeneous 2D elastic plate or shell theory with
plane deformed cross sections, constant elastic transverse
shear strains (the Reissner–Mindlin model), and additionally
at least constant tension/compression strains in the normal
direction. This leads to a 2 12 D theory to avoid numerical
locking phenomena at dimensional transitions from the
coarse 2 12 D to the fine 3D model. The key point is that this
coarse 2 12 D model is supposed to be sufficiently exact in
the major part of the system domain, whereas 3D boundary
layers as well as other disturbances due to local stiffeners,
holes, or concentrated loads are relevant in relatively small
subdomains of the whole system only. This requires the
entire fine 3D model, which usually has elastic, but can
also have elastoplastic, deformations. Another possibility
of 2D–3D model adaptivity is the use of the hierarchical
anisotropic p-variant of the FEM.
As a consequence, dimensional and constitutive model
expansion is necessary in certain subdomains, which, as can
be seen from the examples, become larger for smaller error
tolerances of the a posteriori model and discretization error
estimators.
Another major theoretical and numerical input for efficient
implicit energy-norm error estimators is anisotropic error
testing with higher polynomial order p; p+ = p + 1, p + 2
and smaller mesh size h; h+ = h∕2, h∕4. This is carried out
separately for each of the three coordinate directions of each
element nodal point to account for different stress–strain
behaviors in the tangential and in the normal directions of
the plate/shell. The computational effort is considerably
reduced by performing the described anisotropic error
checks only once on the parametric element level of a structural system (and mapping them to the real isoparametric
element geometry with Jacobian matrices), as well as by
constructing tensor products of the p- and the h-extended
test and trial functions as hierarchical co- and contravariant
orthogonal integrated Legendre polynomials (Stein and
Ohnimus, 1997).
Specifically for the examples in this section, elastic plates
with boundary layers and other irregularities of the thickness and the system geometry are investigated, by which 2D
plate theories become insufficient in those disturbed subdomains. They are supposed to cover relatively small areas of
the whole domain, so that the expansive adaptive modeling
strategy can be effectively applied by switching from the
2 12 D plate to the elastic 3D continuum theory, see Stein and
Ohnimus (1997, 1999, Stein et al. (2007, 2009).
It should be remarked that direct computational modeling
of thin plates in bending with low-order 3D finite elements

69

may yield numerical instabilities and therefore, this is not
recommendable for initial discretizations.
Certainly, model adaptivity, as an important part of validation, see the ASME V&V guide (ASME, 2006), has to be
combined with verification in terms of adaptive discretizations of the plate model and the continuum models denoted
as the coarse and the fine (reference) models, respectively.
Combined a posteriori model and discretization error estimators are thus required, which should be constant free,
at best, and hence usually need implicit postprocessing on
the element or patch levels. In the tradition of our research,
we apply implicit residual-type error estimators using equilibrated residuals by improved C0 -continuous interface
tractions in the normal direction of element boundaries, see
Stein and Ohnimus (1997, 1999).
An important issue is the derivation of upper error bounds
on goal-oriented error measures in terms of (linear or
nonlinear) quantities of interest that depend on the model
and the discretization errors. This requires the construction
of dual problems for the associated model and discretization
estimators, see Stein et al. (2007) and others.
An essential requirement is to achieve the upper-bound
property of the error estimator for the case of dimensional
extensions (e.g., from 2D to 3D problems) in the process of
model adaptivity. The issue in this case is that the necessary
algebraic prolongation from the finite element solution
space of the coarse model to that of the fine model is – of
course – not a Galerkin process and thus does not yield
orthogonality between the error and the test functions. Both
a kinematic and a static method for regaining the orthogonality property, as used for the model error estimator, are
presented in this section.

6.2
6.2.1

2 12 D plate theory and expansion to 3D
continuum theory
Mathematical modeling of thin elastic plates
in bending

Thin-walled engineering structures, such as plates and shells,
are usually modeled hierarchically with simplified 3D theories using problem-oriented hypotheses. In this chapter, the
linear elastic Mindlin model, Mindlin (1951), with the kinematic hypothesis of plane (but not normal) deformations
of cross-section traces is used. This is often addressed as
Reissner–Mindlin plate theory. However, Reissner’s concept
was the assumption of small rotations of the traces of cross
sections and linear bending stresses along the plate thickness as well and applying a mixed variational principle, see
Reissner (1947). For detailed studies we refer to Models and
Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Many researchers investigated the problem of whether
the Kirchhoff plate theory (with normality hypothesis
yielding the inhomogeneous bipotential PDE) and the
Reissner–Mindlin plate theory (admitting averaged, i.e.,
constant transverse shear deformations along the plate thickness t) are hierarchical approximations of the 3D elasticity
theory for t → 0. The question whether these models are
asymptotic for small thickness gained interest again in Braess
et al. (2011), based on the work of Morgenstern (1959) who
introduced the ansatz for the transverse deflection of the plate
in Cartesian coordinates as u3 (x, y, z) = w(x, y) + z2 W(x, y),
that is, with a quadratic term in the z-direction. Neglecting
this term yields an asymptotic model error of the order
1
(𝛼t 2 ), with the transverse shear correction factor 𝛼. With
the term z2 W(x, y), the shear correction factor can be determined using the Prager-Synge two-energy principle, as
outlined in Section 5.2.7, depending on Poisson’s ratio 𝜈. In
the theory by Arnold et al. (2002) and the extension in Braess
et al. (2011), it was proven that – for the case of clamped
edges – the difference of the model errors of the Kirchhoff
and Reissner–Mindlin theories become asymptotically small
1
for thin plates with the order (t 2 ).
A major problem in model adaptivity is the dimensional
transition from 2D plate to 3D continuum theory and especially the coupling of related finite elements. Direct coupling
requires transition elements for avoiding thickness locking
but they need big efforts in the data management. Therefore, the so-called 2 12 D isoparametric quadrilateral elements
with linear test and trial functions uz,h , vz,h along the thickness are introduced, yielding constant strains in the thickness
direction and thus avoiding thickness locking, as discussed
in Section 6.2.2. For this reason, all 2D elements used in this
section are topologically 3D elements. The same holds for
1D beam elements.

6.2.2

The 2 12 D extended Reissner–Mindlin elastic
plate theory and 3D theory of linearized
elasticity

6.3
6.3.1

Goal-oriented coupled model
and discretization error estimators
Concepts for error-controlled model
and discretization adaptivity realizing
computational verification and validation

According to the verification and validation code (V&V
Code) of ASME from 2006 (ASME, 2006), the requirements

71

of verification and validation have to be fulfilled in all industrial and technological areas. Verification means calculating
the given model right, that is, approximating the true solution with a prescribed error tolerance of an adequate bounded
error norm, and validation requires the calculation of the
right model. This implies model adaptivity in subdomains
from coarse to fine models in related subdomains as investigated in our group, see Stein and Ohnimus (1997, 1999),
Stein et al. (2007, 2009).
In the group of J.T. Oden, the articles (Oden and Cho, 1996;
Oden et al., 2001; Oden and Prudhomme, 2002) with basic
concepts for model adaptivity are emphasized.
In Figure 34, a flow chart of combined adaptive modeling,
computations, and physical experiments shows the whole
process of computational and experimental verification and
the interaction of both for validation.
The example, Figures 35 and 36, motivates expansive
model adaptivity, coupled with mesh adaptivity for verification in each step of model adaptivity, see Stein and
Ohnimus (1997, 1999). Figure 35 shows adaptive model
expansion from 2 12 D plate theory to 3D continuum theory in
the cutout of a continuous square plate with haunches above
the supporting columns (a) and the bending stresses 𝜎xx at the
cutout (b) for the coarse and the fine model. The discretization error estimator requires a smaller tolerance than the
model error estimator, for example, with a factor 2 or 3. It is
obvious that a prescribed smaller tolerance of the fine model
error yields larger subdomains of the fine model.

6.3.2

Prolongations and error definitions

Summarizing the variational forms in Section 6.2.2, it should
be clear that we obtain another type of error in addition to
the well-known discretization error, which results from the
discretization of the coarse model by the FEM. This new type
of error results from the approximation of the fine model by
the coarse model and therefore is termed as model error or
modeling error.
Although other definitions are possible, here we define the
model error as
m+1 ∋ eu,mod = um+1 − um

(439)

that is, in terms of the solutions of the fine and the coarse
model. Since, as mentioned above, m ⊄ m+1 , we introduced a prolongation operator  ∶ m → m+1 to map
displacements from the (coarse) 2 12 D model to the (fine) 3D
model.
The discretization error is defined as in (231) as
m ∋ eu,m,dis = um − um,h

6.3.3

Error measures

When it comes to practical engineering computations,
goal-oriented error measures, such as the classical energy
norm or – more challenging in practice – the deflection of a
plate or the bending stress at a specific point, are of interest.

quantities of interest Qm+1 ∶ m+1 → ℝ and Qm ∶ m → ℝ.

linear functionals.

Algorithmic aspects

Due to the complexity of the adaptive process, we present a
condensed sequence of all algorithmic steps.
First, the primal and dual discrete problems of the coarse
problem (436) and (453), respectively, are solved globally with finite elements. Usually, isoparametric biquadratic
quadrilateral 2D elements and linear shape functions in the
transverse direction will be used for the coarse model. Note

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

Finite Element Methods for Elasticity

that in the first adaptive step, the fine model is not yet
present.
∗
Once the finite element solutions um,h and um,h are determined and the tractions are equilibrated on the element level
(with element patches around each element node) for both
the primal and the dual problems, the local Neumann problems (482) and (483) associated with the discretization errors
∗
eu,m,dis and eu,m,dis
, respectively, are solved approximately
with the finite element method using test and trial functions
with p+ = p + 1, p + 2, that is, two orders higher than the
shape functions used for the original finite element solution.
In order to approximately estimate the model error on each
coarse element, we need a (fine) 3D finite element solution with the bilinear form of the 3D model m + 1 and thus
with the constitutive equation of the fine model. These 3D
solutions are obtained locally by the prolongated 2 12 D solutions yielding a locally approximated 3D solution. With this
3D solution at hand, we then solve the Neumann problems
in the higher model (474) and (480) associated with the
∗
model errors eu,mod and eu,mod
, respectively, using anisotropic
higher order test and trial functions again with p+ = p +
1, p + 2.
Instead of these locally approximated model errors, one
could add a further global finite element solution with the
provisionally model-adapted 3D elements. Then one would
be able to compute a more accurate local model error estimator, but this is computationally too expensive and not
necessary.
It is now possible to estimate the error measure Emod,dis
by computing the error estimates (471) or (473). If the error
exceeds a given tolerance on the element level, the element
will be refined/model expanded. In the case of the discretization error, the element will be subdivided into four elements
using hanging nodes, whereas in the case of the model error,
the 2 12 D element will be replaced by a 3D element.
Upon refining/expanding the mesh, the algorithm
presented above will be repeated starting with global
primal and dual solutions of the updated subdomains of
both the coarse and the fine model. For the fine model,
isoparametric quadratic hexahedral elements will be used.

6.4

Prolongation of coarse-to-fine model solutions

As mentioned earlier, an admissible definition of the model
error in the case of dimensional model adaptivity requires
the prolongation of the finite element solutions of the coarse
model into the finite element solution space of the fine model
kin
using the (kinematic) matrix m+1,m
with dimension (m +
1) × m.

6.4.1

Orthogonalization of kinematic prolongations

As we have seen in Section 6.3.5, the prolongation operator
 has to fulfill (469) in order to derive an orthogonality
relation for the model error eu,mod as shown in (470). For
this purpose, we introduce a premultiplied orthogonal matrix
 ort . In discretized form, the orthogonality condition (469)
has to be fulfilled for each element yielding the following
orthogonality condition on the element level:
am+1,e (h,e um,h,e , h,e vh,e ) = Fm+1,e (h,e vh,e )

6.5

Numerical example: haunched T-beam
supported on single columns

6.5.1

Kinematic prolongation from the coarse to the
fine model

The kinematic prolongation of nodal displacements from the
coarse plate model m to the fine continuum element m + 1 is
visualized in Figure 37.
Horizontal nodal displacements in the x and y directions are
introduced at the upper and lower surfaces as DOF – instead
of rotation angles 𝜑x , 𝜑y – and linear displacements in the
z-direction. The nonreduced kinematic prolongation from the
biquadratic 2 12 D plate to the triquadratic 3D element reads

Orthogonalization of static prolongations

An alternative strategy to compute a prolongated solution
with the desired orthogonality properties is to solve a local
variational problem on the element level, which is also based
on the orthogonality condition (469). The key idea is to introduce a static prolongation of the external loads rather than a
kinematic prolongation of the displacements as introduced in
the preceding section.

The T-beam considered in this example is part of a bridge,
stiffened by a haunched truss at the beam axis and supported
by equidistant columns, Figure 39. For the coarse 2 12 D plate
model m, biquadratic Lagrangian test functions are used
and for the fine 3D model m + 1, triquadratic polynomials,
respectively, see Figure 37. Figure 40 shows the points in
which vertical displacements as quantities of interest and
associated dual vertical loads at these points are calculated.
Figure 41 depicts the primal and dual deformations for
the deflection at point A and at point B as the quantities
of interest. Three scenarios have been calculated for each
of the dual problems: (i) discretization adaptivity with 2 12 D
elements only, (ii) expansive model and discretization adaptivity from 2 12 D to 3D modeling, and (iii) discretization adaptivity with 3D elements only.
Figure 42 presents the deflection at point B as the quantity
of interest of the coupled model and discretization error
estimators for the pure 2D Reissner–Mindlin model, the
coarse enhanced 2 12 D model m, and the fine 3D model m + 1.
As can be seen, the model error can only be reduced by the
transition from the coarse to the fine model. The convergence
rate corresponds to the a priori rate of the polynomial order.
Adaptivity for quantities of interest (deflections) at points
A,B, and C, Figure 40, is realized first with nonguaranteed upper-bound but efficient error estimators for point C,
Figure 43. The convergence rate for using model adaptivity
from 2 12 D to 3D model is obviously better than using 3D
elements only. Guaranteed and nonguaranteed upper-bound
error estimators are examined. Evidently, the results for the
nonguaranteed error estimator are more accurate and efficient. Mesh adaptivity of the coarse model m decreases the
model error not below 8%, whereas expansive model adaptivity with model m + 1 yields a coupled error estimator of
1% with 20 000 DOF.

Model and discretization adaptivity

GOAL-ORIENTED A POSTERIORI
ERROR ESTIMATION IN FINITE
ELASTICITY

In this section, we leave the theory of linearized elasticity
and turn our attention to a posteriori error estimators in
finite elasticity. As we will see, upon introducing a suitable
linearization of the nonlinear problem, many of the basic
techniques to estimate the discretization error, as presented in
Section 5, can be transferred to the nonlinear case. However,
attention must be paid, since a counterpart of the energy
norm does not exist in finite elasticity. As a consequence,
our objective in this section is primarily to determine how
goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators can be derived

We begin our studies of error estimators for the finite elasticity problem by establishing – similarly to the derivations by Eriksson et al. (1995) and Becker and Rannacher
(1996) – the fundamentally important Galerkin orthogonality relation and an exact error residual equation for
the finite element discretization error eu obtained while
discretizing the nonlinear variational problem (89). As it
turns out, error representations for the linearized elasticity
problem, as presented in Section 5.1.1, are included as a
special case.
Let us first recall the weak form of the residual (108). It
is important to note that a is now a semilinear form rather
than a bilinear form, which is the essential difference to
the derivations in Section 5. In particular, from the discrete
variational problem (105), we again deduce that the weak
form of the residual vanishes for all functions vh ∈ h , that
is,
Ru (vh ) = a(u, vh ) − a(uh , vh ) = 0

If one is to solve (507) numerically with FEM in order to
obtain an approximation of eu , it turns out that two seemingly unsurmountable problems arise. First, approximations
of the discretization error eu in the finite element spaces h
cannot be computed because of the Galerkin orthogonality
(504). Secondly, the bilinear form aS is not computable in the
sense that it involves the unknown exact solution u. We can at
least practically overcome the second drawback by approximating the exact solution. An obvious choice is to replace
u with the associated finite element solution uh . In doing
this in (506), we can introduce the tangent bilinear form
aT ∶  ×  → ℝ at the finite element solution uh defined

Let us remark that with the Newton–Raphson algorithm,
as presented in Section 3.2, it is easily verified that if
the solution uh ∈ h to the discrete problem (105) is iterated with sufficient accuracy in terms of vanishingly small
residuals, the discretization error eu ∈  is the solution
of an additional Newton step in an infinite-dimensional
space.

Duality techniques and error representations

Arguing in the same way as in Section 5.5.2, we next introduce the dual problem in order to derive an error representation formula for the goal-oriented error measure E within
the framework of finite elasticity. Since the derivation of the
dual problem and the error representation formula is essentially the same as for the linearized elasticity problem in
Section 5.5.2, we only summarize the main results.
In the case of finite elasticity, the dual problem reads as

7.1.3

The general strategy for a posteriori error
estimation

Now that we have at our disposal the error representation
formulas (519) and (520), we may, as in Section 5.5.4,
establish a general concept for goal-oriented a posteriori
error estimation in finite elasticity.
It needs to be emphasized that at first glance, it seems as
if the bilinear form aT induces a norm on  (with r = 2),
since the finite element solution uh is piecewise polynomial and thus implies C(uh ), c(uh ) ∈ [L∞ (Ω)]3×3×3×3 and
S(uh ), 𝝉(uh ) ∈ [L∞ (Ω)]3×3 . Hence, the tensors C(uh ) and
c(uh ) as well as S(uh ) and 𝝉(uh ) operate as fixed coefficients.
Consequently, aT (v, v) is computable in the sense that the
integrals exist for all v ∈ . However, because of the error
in uh , the evaluation of the integrals might lead to negative
results for some v ∈ . In other words, the positive definiteness of the tangent form aT cannot be maintained for
arbitrary v ∈ . The conclusion to be drawn is that the usage
of aT has to be bypassed when it comes to the application
of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the a posteriori error
analysis.
To cope with this problem, as in Section 5.2.5, we introduce
Ritz projections of the true error eu . More precisely, for
“small” errors eu , we define the Ritz projections eu , eu∗ ∈ 
of the exact finite element discretization errors eu and eu∗ ,
respectively, as follows

(517)

a𝜋 (̄eu , v) = aT (eu , v)

∀v ∈ 

(521a)

∀v ∈ 

(521b)

for any 𝜋h u ∈ h . Note that in the special case where 𝜋h u =

Here, a𝜋 ∶  ×  → ℝ denotes a not necessarily symmetric
but continuous and -elliptic bilinear form. For the time
being, the bilinear form a𝜋 may be chosen arbitrarily.
We note that Ritz projections have been frequently used
in the literature to obtain more tractable problems in the a
posteriori error analysis, especially for mixed problems. As

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. in (nearly) incompressible finite hyperelasticity was introduced by Brink and Stein (1998); see also Araya et al. (2002)
for further error estimators for nonlinear elliptic problems.
Moreover, a goal-oriented a posteriori error estimator for the
Stokes and Oseen equations, which rests upon a Ritz projection similar to (522) was presented by Oden and Prudhomme
(1999).

formula (519) now becomes

(522)

Recall that alternatively, the error eu can be exactly represented by means of the weak form of the residual as we have
seen in (520). However, the Ritz projections eu and eu∗ cannot
be involved in this manner.

A posteriori error estimators for FEM

This section is devoted to a systematic derivation of finite
element error estimation techniques for goal-oriented error
measures in finite elasticity. Our aim here is to present again
three principally different strategies covering the classes of
residual-, hierarchical-, and gradient averaging-type error
estimators. All estimators rely on the general strategy as
presented in the previous section.

7.2.2

Hierarchical-type error estimators based
on local Dirichlet problems

Let us now derive a hierarchical goal-oriented a posteriori
error estimator for the finite elasticity problem as elaborated
in Larsson et al. (2002) for materially nonlinear elasticity.
Recalling the strategy as presented in Section 5.6.2, it turns
out that in finite elasticity, the error estimator can be derived
in essentially the same way as for the linearized elasticity
problem.
We will begin our studies by transferring the saturation
assumption (396) to the case of nonlinear problems, that

7.2.3

Gradient averaging-type error estimators

Now that we have at our disposal several residual-type
goal-oriented a posteriori error estimators and two
hierarchical-type error estimators in finite elasticity, we
next show how recovery techniques can be used in order to
derive goal-oriented error estimators for the finite elasticity
problem. The notion of recovery techniques was already
explained in Sections 5.2.6 and 5.6.3. As we will see below,
principally the same techniques can be applied in the case of
the finite elasticity problem.
A key issue in obtaining a sharp estimate of the error
measure E is to compute good approximations of the
discretization errors on the element level. It was already
shown in Section 5.6.3 that these error approximations can be
expressed in terms of approximations of the stress error, that
is, a gradient error in a more general setting, for the primal
∗
as defined in
and the dual problems given by 𝝌 𝜎,e and 𝝌 𝜎,e
(309) and (404), respectively. The link between the stress
error e𝜎 and the displacement error eu was provided by the
bilinear form associated with the complementary energy
(95) with corresponding complementary energy norm (96).
As mentioned earlier, however, in finite elasticity, an energy
norm does not exist. To cope with this problem, we recall
the error estimator (528) and try to approximate the local
weak form of the residual Ru,e instead, as was presented in
Rüter and Stein (2004), to estimate the error of the J-integral
in non-LEFMs.
Recalling the definition of the weak form of the residual
Ru , as presented in (108), as well as the definition of the
semilinear form a, as given in (88b), we recognize that the
local form Ru,e is given by

In this numerical example, we investigate a compressed
foam rubber tube. Owing to the compression, rather high
horizontal displacements arise. Our aim here is therefore to
control the error of these horizontal displacements.
The system is an isotropic tube in plane-strain state with
radius r = 20 mm subjected to a vertical compression load
F. Owing to symmetry conditions, only one quarter of the
system is modeled, as shown in Figure 45. The chosen
specific strain-energy function is of the neo-Hookean type as
presented in Section 2.4. For the (compressible) foam rubber,
the following material data are assumed: 𝜆 = 576.92 N mm−2
and 𝜇 = 384.62 N mm−2 , which corresponds to E = 1000
N mm−2 and 𝜈 = 0.3. The primal load F = 14 N mm−2 is
applied in two load steps. In this example, the finite element
subspace h is constructed using isoparametric Q1 elements,
as introduced in Section 3.3.5.

As adumbrated above, our objective in this example is to
control the error in the (mean) horizontal displacements at
the right-hand (and left-hand) side of the tube. To this end,
the goal quantity of interest Q is chosen as
Q(u) =

1
𝝀 ⋅ u dA
|ΓN1 | ∫ΓN1

(558)

The locations of the boundary ΓN1 and the unit vector 𝝀
representing the direction of interest can be gathered from
Figure 45. A reference solution of the mean horizontal
displacements is obtained using isoparametric Q2 elements
with 137 872 DOF in total and yields Q(u) = 4.9 196 043 316
mm. Note that adaptive mesh refinements have been used in
order to obtain a highly accurate reference solution.
The convergence behavior of the error estimators presented
can be seen in Figure 46. All estimators yield approximately the same optimal convergence rate. However, the
residual-type error estimators produce higher values for
the estimated error measure than the hierarchical- and the
gradient averaging-type estimators.
Apparently, the fact that the convergence curves of the
residual-type error estimators are on top of the ones obtained
by the hierarchical as well as the gradient averaging error
estimators is reflected in terms of higher effectivity indices
as depicted in Figure 47. Furthermore, it becomes obvious
that all residual error estimators yield upper bounds on the
error measure E, although from theory only “residual 4” is
a guaranteed upper bound; cf. Section 7.2.1. Moreover, the
hierarchy of the estimators as given in (378a) can be verified.

The values obtained between 1.5 and 4 are relatively sharp
compared to other numerical examples in finite elasticity
based on these error estimators. This relies on the fact that
in the numerical example considered, the angle between the
true errors eu and eu∗ is comparatively small. In contrast, the
hierarchical error estimators and the gradient averaging error
estimators do not yield bounds on the error measure E, but
much sharper error estimates with an effectivity around the
desired value of one.

8

CONCLUDING REMARKS

The major issue of this chapter concerns deterministic verification and validation of classical FEMs and major generalizations for BVPs of linearized and finite elastic deformations in structural C1 continua under static loads. This
is based on a posteriori error estimators and the related
adaptivity of finite element approximation spaces to achieve
optimal convergence rates and computational efficiency.
Next to classical FEMs with test and trial polynomials in
suitable subdomains, error estimations, and adaptivity for
generalized direct variational methods are presented, especially the XFEM and the meshfree RKPM. Also a section on
the important Finite Element Exterior Calculus by Arnold
et al. is included.
Besides the rigorous presentation of the whole methodology and the algorithms – particularly consistent linearizations of variational equations and of error measures – the

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2025 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

90

systematic comparative treatment of the following four types
of a posteriori error estimators for verification is covered,
amended by suitable examples:
1.

2.

3.
4.

Explicit and implicit residual-type error estimators
based on weak residuals and solutions of local Neumann
problems on the finite element level subjected to
equilibrated residuals.
Hierarchical-type error estimators based on the solutions
of Dirichlet problems on element patches in hierarchically enhanced test and solution spaces.
Gradient averaging-type error estimators using the
recovery of displacement gradient fields.
Constitutive equation error estimators based on statically
admissible stress fields.

Goal-oriented error estimators for the desired
goal-quantities of engineering interest are of great practical importance. These estimators require the solution of
auxiliary dual problems, which are always linear – even in
the case of finite elasticity. In this chapter, the goal quantity of
interest is, among others, defined as the nonlinear J-integral,
the condition for static crack propagation in the framework
of finite and linearized elasticity. The errors of all goal quantities of interest, presented here, are estimated by the four
different types of error estimation techniques mentioned
above for both mesh-based and meshfree methods. It
should be pointed out that upper and lower error bounds can
only be obtained by residual-type and the related constitutive
equation error estimators, whereas hierarchical- and gradient
averaging-type error estimators yield sharp error estimates,
with the disadvantage that no error bounds can be obtained.
Moreover, validation requires that coupled error estimation of the discretization error of a given mathematical model
together with the modeling and dimensional errors in crucial
subdomains – with respect to a hierarchically more accurate
model – is essential for reliable and efficient modeling in
conjunction with adaptive numerical approximations of the
mechanical behavior of engineering structures. There is no
doubt that both the discretization and the modeling error
should be of equal order of magnitude for a consistent finite
element analysis, and thus for the safety and reliability of
engineering structures, also providing engineering insight
and reasoning.
